INDIAN STANDARDS INSTITUTION 
New Delhi, 1981*05-14 

S. O. 1658. — In pursuance of sub-rule (1) of rule 4 of the Indian Standards Institution (Certification Marks) Rules, 1955 the Indian 
Standards Institution, hereby, notifies that the Standard Mark(s), design(s) of which together with the verbal description of the design(s) 
and tin title(s) of the relevant Indian Standard(s) are given in tho Schedule hereto annexed, have been specified.

SO 1 665 /IDRA/6/4/ 78 — In exercise of the powers 
confeued by section 6 of the Industries (Development and 
Regulation) Act, 1951 (65 of 1951), read with rule s 2 and 4 
of the Development Councils (Proccduial) Rules, 1952, the 
Central Government hereby appoints Dr S, Vardurajan, Chair- 
man and Managing Director, Indian Petrochemicals Corpo- 
ration Ltd , Baroda (Gujarat), a member of the Development 
Council for Organic Chemical Industries, to be the Vice- 
Chairman of the said Development Council and directs that 
the following amendments shall be made in the Order of 
the Government of India m the Ministry of Industry (Depart- 
ment of Industual Development) No. S O /IDRA/6/4/78, 
dated the 30th October, 1979, namely ;— 

(i) after SI No 1 and the entues relating thereto the 
following shall be inserted, namely ■ — 

“2 Dr S. Vardarajan, 


Chairman & Managing Director, 


“Vice Chairman” 


Indian Petro-Chemicala 
Corporation Ltd , Baroda (Gujarat), 

(ii) S No 7 and entries relating thereto shall be omitted, 

(iii) S Nos 2, 3, 4, J and 6 shall be re-numbered at 

SI, Nos. 3, 4, 5, 6 and 7 rcspectit ely. 

[No, 8(3)/74-CDN] 
K K. JASWAL, Dy. Secy.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 19th May > 19S1 

S.Ch 16<96/lDRA/6/5, — In exerciso of the powers con- 
ferred by section 6 of the Industries (Development and 
Regulation) Act, 1951 (65 of 1951), read with mlcs 2, 4 and 
5 of the Development Council (Procedural) Rules, 1952, the 
Central Government hereby appoints, for a period of two 
years, on and from the date or publication of this Order in 
the Official Gazette, the following persons to he members 
of the Development Council for scheduled industries engaged 
in the manufacture or production of Food Processing Incuts- 
tries in place of members appointed under the Order of the 
Government of India in the erstwhile Ministiy of Induslty 
and Civil Supplies, No. S.O. 313-IDRA, dated the 10th Janu- 
ary, 1975, whose term of office haa expired by efflux df 
time or otherwise : — 

Development Council of Food Processing Industrial

MINISTRY OF PETROLEUM, CHEMICAIS Sc 
FERTILIZER 

(Department of Petroleum) 

New Delhi, the 20th May, 1981 

S.O, 1667. — Whereas it appears to the Central Govern- 
ment that it is necessary in the public interest that for the 
transport of petroleum products from Mathura in Uttar 
Pradesh to Jullundur in Punjab pipelines should be laid by 
the Indian Oil Corporation Limited. 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such, 
pipelines, it is necessary to acquire the Right of User In the 
land described in the schedule annexed hereto : 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
Sub-section (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals 
pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declare its in- 
tention to acquire the right of user therein : 

Piovidcd that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification, object to 
the laying of the pipelines under the land to the Competent 
Authority, Indian Oil Corporation Limited, Mathura-Jullundur 
Pipeline New House, Kunjpura Road, Rarnal (Haryana)* 

And every person making such an objection shall also state 
specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person or by a 
legal practitioner.

1817 


S,0, 1668- — Whereas it appears to tho Central Govorn- 
mont that it is necessary in tho public interest that for tho trans- 
port of petroleum products from Mathura in Uttar Pradesh to 
Jullundur in Punjab, pipelines should be laid by tho Indian Oil 
Corporation Limited, 

And whereas it appears for tho purpose of laying such pipe- 
lines, it is necessary to acquire the Right of User in tho land des- 
cribed in the schedule annexed hereto : 

Now therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub- 
setion (1) of section 3 of the Petroleum and Minerals pipelines 
(Acquisition of Right of User in land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), 
the Central Government hereby declare its intention to acquire 
the right of user therein : 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the dato of this notification, object to tho 
laying of the pipelines under the land to tho Competent Autho- 
rity, Indian Oil Corporation Limited, Mathura-Jullundur 
Pipeline 705, Mota Singh Nagar, Jullundur. 

And every person making such an objection shall also state 
specifically whether lie wishs to be heard in person or by a legal 
practitioner.

Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govern- 
ment Industrial Tribunal No. 2, Dhanbad, in the Industrial 
dispute between the employers in relation to the management 
of Tetulmari Colliery of Messrs Bharat Coking Coal Limited, 
Post Office Sijua, District Dhanbad, a ad their workmen which 
wis received by the Central Government on the 12th May, 
1981.

AWARD 

This 1§ a reference under S. 10 of the I. D, Act, 194V. 
The Central Government by its notification No, L~20012(44) / 
80-D. HI(A) dated 4th September, 1980 has referred Oils 
dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication on the following 
terms : 

SCHEDULE 

"Whether the demand of the workmen of Tetulmari cot- 
liery of Messrs Bharat Coking Coal limited. Post 
Office Sijua, District Dhanbad that Shri Mahabir 
Saw, Munshi should be reinstated and paid wages 
for the period of idleness from the 30th November, 
1976 h justified 7 If so, to what relief is the said 
workman entitled?” 

2. After receipt of the reference notices were sent to both 
the parties for filing their written statements and documents. 
Both parties took some time for filing he settlement. Ultimately 
today a memorandum of settlement signed on 16-12-80 was 
filed by the parties Incorporating therein the terms of settle- 
ment arrived at between them in respect of the industrial 
dispute pending for adjudication in this Tribunal. As per 
the settlement Shri Mahabir Saw the concerned workman

would be taken ba;k in employment without any back wages 
In the same job in which he wus working previously. Since

Short recital of the case 

Shri Mahabir Sao was working as Surface Munshi in 
Tctulmari colliery since take over of the colliery by the Cen- 
tral Government in 1976. One Shri Basaval was detected 
to have been working in the name of Mahabir Sao, The 
Imposter found so working was stopped by the management 
vide letter No. TC/73 /20/Pers/76 dated 29-11-76 from, the 
Superintendent, Tctulmari colliery. However, the genuine per- 
son Mahabir Sao who was absenting was not available for 
duty. Subsequently the union raised! dispute that the genuine 
person Mahabir Sao was also stopped from work and de- 
manded that he should be taken back in employment. The 
matter could not be resolved in course of conciliation pro- 
ceedings. Subsequently the union raised dispute again before 
the General Manager which was taken up for discussion in 
5-8-80 with Shri Bindeshwarl Dubey, President RCMS,. In 
course of discussion it was agreed that if the management 
would fail to approve that Shri Mahabir Sao as an importer 
witWn one month then the claimant should be taken back 
In employment. Accordingly the case of Shri Mahabir Sao 
was referred to the District Magistrate Dhanbad vide this 
office Jetter No, OM/SA/PD/43 ( /5379|80 dated 20(26-8-80 
for Verification In order to prove identity of the person claim- 
ing to be Mahabir Sao. But so far nothing has been 
heard from the District Magistrate In this regard. On the 
other hand the union have been pressing for allowing Shri 
Mahabir Sao in terms of management's commitment in course 
of Union Management negotiation on 5-8-80. The matter 
was again taken up for discussion with the Union and after 
discussion it was agreed to take Shri Mahabir Sao back in 
employment temporarily on the following terms and con- 
ditions. 


Term* erf Settlement 

(1) That the pemon claiming to be Mahabir Sao would 
be taken back in employment in the same job in 
which he was working last. 

(2) That after receipt of police verification report from 
the Dy, Commissioner, Dhanbad if it is found that 
the person who is hereby allowed to resume duty 
is not actually Mahabir Sao he would be stopped 
from work without any reference to the workman 
concerned. 


(3) That the workman concerned will produce, for th< 
time being, certificate from the concerned Blocl 
Development Officer regarding his real identity am 
would also submit an affidavit duly executed before 
the 1st Class Magistrate. 

(4) That the concerned workman would not be entitle* 

perlirf ^ Wa * efl or whatsoever, for the pas 


(5) That the workman concerned would report for duty 
within 10 days from the date of this settlement a loog 
with B DO-cert ifleate, affidavit and duly attested 
photograph in triplicate, 

(6) That this will not be cited as a precedent in res- 
pect of any subsequent dispute 

(7) That this settlement shall be registered under Rule 
58(4) of the Industrial Dispute (Central) Rules, 
1957. 


(P. R. Sinha)

S.O, 1682.— In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial Dis- 
pute* Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Government hereby 
publishes the following award of the Central Government 
Industrial Tribunal No. 1, Dhanbad, in the industrial dispute 
between the employers In relation to the management of Bhu- 
lanbararee Colliery of Messrs Bharat Coking Coal Limited, 
Post Office Patherdih (Dhanbad) and their workmen, which was 
leceivcd by the Central Government on the 15th May, 1981. 


BEFORE THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUS- 
TRIAL TRIBUNAL NO. 1. DHANBAD 

(In the matter of a reference under Sec. 10(1) ifd) of the 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947) 

Reference No. 26 of 1977 

PARTIES : 

Employers in relation to the Bhulanbaiaree Colliery of 
Messrs Bharat Coking Coal Limited, Post Office 
Patherdih (Dhanbad ) 

AND 

Their Workman, 

APPEARANCES : 

For the Employers : 

Shri G. Prasad, Advocate. 

CATE 

For the Workman ; 

Shri S. Bose, Secretary, Colliery Mazdoor Sangh. Dhan- 
bad. 

STATE ; Bihar INDUSTRY : Coal 

Dhanbad, the 8th May, 1981 


AWARD 

By Order No. D20012/158/7 5-D. IH/A. dated the 2nd 
Februray, 1976, the Central Govt, being of opinion that 
an Industrial dispute existed between the employers in re- 
lation to Bhulanb&raree Colliery of M/s. Bharat Coking 
Coal Ltd. and their workman in respect of the matter 
specified in the schedule attached t 0 the order referred 
the same for adjudication to Industrial Tribunal No, 2 
Dhanbad. While the matter was pending there the refe- 
T i C ^ r to th * s Tribunal vide Order No, S- 

1 »° 2 u ( i j/77 ' ( L ,)_DlVfB) dated 22-2-1977, The uchedul# 
attached to the order reads thus, 

“Whether the action of the management of Bhulan- 
bararee Colliery of M Bharat Coking Coal 
Limited P.O Patherdih, Distt. DhnnbadTn dis- 
missing S/shri G. P. Choudhury, U,S, Tiwary and 



1832 


[Part II— Sue. 3(ii)J 


THE OAZETTL OF INDIA JUNE 


Sri Jalim Ram, all Munshies with effect from 
21-4-1975, is justified? If not, to what relief are 
they entitled?” 

2. After notice to the parties they have filed their respec- 
tive written statements In addition to it* written state- 
ment the union as filed a rejoinder of the written statement 
of the management. 

3, The case of the management as revealed from its written 

statement is that between 1-4-74 to 4-5-74 the concerned 
workmen were underground munshis engaged in the main- 
dip at 10 Seam in the colliery in question. Duripg this period 
pick coal was being raised from the main dip. The total i 
raising of pick coal reported by the three concerned workmen 
was 181 tubs whereas according to dimention of the drivage in 
the main dip during trie relevant period the raising of pick 
coal should have been only 52.5 tuba. For this over reporting 
the concerned! workmen were charged on 27-6-74 and they 
submitted their replies on 12-7-74. The explanations submitted 
by them not being and satisfactory the management held a 
domestic enquiry regarding the charges against the three 
concerned workmen. The common enquiry was held on 
2IF9-1974, 23-9-1974 and 9-10-1974 in presence of the 

concerned workmen who cross-examined the management's 
witnesses but did not produce any evidence in support of their 
defence. After the closure of the enquiry the Enquiry Officer 
submitted his report on 20-11-1974 in which after cloborate 
discussion of the evidence he held the workman to be guifiy 
of the charges. As there was no extenuating c ire um stance in 
favour of the workmen and as the charges were of serious 
nature the General Manager and Chief Mining Engineer 
approved the findings and recommendation of dismissal of the 
Enquiry Officer. Accordingly the workmen were dismissed 
on 21-4-1975. The management acted bonafidc all along. 
In view of seriousness of the charge and <in the interest of 
proper maintenance of discipline in the colliery the punishment 
of dismissal imposed by the management on the three concer- 
ned workmen is fully justified, and does not call for any 
interference. 

4. The case of the union- as made out in its written statement 
may be briefly stated thus. The three workmen were perma- 
nent employees of Bhulanbararee Colliery and were working 
as underground immshis in mair* dip 10 Seam during the rele- 
vant period ic. from 1-4-74 to 4*5-74. The charges framed 
against the workmen are vague, indefinite and false. On receiv- 
ing the chargesheets the concerned workmen approached the 
management for supply of relevant papers and of copies of 
the Standing Orders. The management refused to supply the 
same, thus depriving them of their inberant right to show cause 
against the chargesheets levelled against them The manage- 
ment also did not give to the concerned workman full opportu- 
nity to submit their explanations to the charges. The 
charpesheet against the concerned workmen were not issued 
by competent authority. The chargesheets were not accom- 
panied by complete statements of allegations against the 
workmen and were not supported by any document to enable 
the concerned workmen to understand the charges agaist them. 
The management before ascertaining the dimenlion of the 
drivage in the main dip 10 Seam could not have chargesheetcxj 
the concerned workmen. As the progress in the main dip 10 
Seam 7th level junction was hampered due to water trouble 
during the relevant period, the Asstt. Colliery Manager and 
Overman of the CoMiery in question asked the miners of the 
main dip to raise coal from North Air Crossing from North 
level and from South level and to show the coal thus raised 
as pick coal raised from main dip. Accordingly by the three 
concerned workmen booked pick coal raised by the miners 
from the aforesaid three places m pick coal raised from main 
dip. The reporting thus made by the concerned workmen as 
per the instructions of the Asst. Colliery Manager and Over- 
man was checked and approved bv the Assff Colliery Manager. 
So no fault Cftuld be found with the concerned workmen. After 
the workmen submitted their explanations the decision of the 
management to hold a domestic enquiry was takei so suddenly 
thdt there was no time left for the concerned workmen to 
object to the appointment of the Enquiry Officer who was 
directed to hold enquiry. Tn the enquiry that followed the 
concerned workmen were not given opportunity to engage a 
defence helper. The enquiry was held In such a perfuncorv 
planner that the concerned workmen could not get opportunity 
to examine witness in support of their defence. As three 
different chargesheets were issued against the three different 


6, 1 98 1/J Y AISTHA 16, 1903 


workmen the joint enquiry held by the Enquiry Officer for all 
the three chargesheets has prejudiced the concerned workmen 
seriously. The Manager ol the colliery wu£ all along present 
in the enquiry to exercise his influence over the enquhyi officer. 

1 he managers presence in the enquiry also influenced the 
witnesses who were examined in the euquiry. The Enquiry 
Officer did not give reasonable and full opportunity to the 
concerned workmen either to produce their own documents or 
to call for certain documents or to examine witnesses in support

stock register which would have shown that there was no over- 
reporting as alleged against the concerned workmen. The 
Enquiry Officer should have held that officers other than the 
three concerned workmen had also their responsibility in the 
alleged over-reporting, The charge of mis-appropnation 
against the concerned workmen is absolutely unfounded. 
The payments made to the miners being duly scrutinised 
and certified by Asstt. Colliery Manager the management’s 
case that the three concerned workmen mis- appropriated 
any money by alleged over-reporting cannot stand. The 
order of dismissal passed against the concerned workmen 
ie not only malafide but is also not justified. Hence tfao con- 
cerned workmen are entitled to be reinstated. 


5, As the union in its written statement challenged the 
fairness of the domestic enquiry at the instance of the parties 
the question of fairness of domestic enquiry was determined 
by my predecessor. By order dated 31st May, 1977 my pre- 
decessor after scrutinising the evidence led by the parties 
and after hearing them held that the domestic enquiry was 
fair and proper in so far as TJ. S. Tiwary and Jalim Ram 
were concerned and the same was not fair and proper In so 
far as G. P. Choudhury was concerned. It is on account 
of this order the management in course of bearing of the case 
before me led fresh evidence by examining four witnesses, 
namely, MW-2, to MW-5 to prove its case against G, P. 
Choudhury. Sri G. P, Choudhury one of the concerned work- 
men also led evidence in course of the hearing to rebut 
the evidence led by the management examining himself only 
as WW-2. Besides the aforesaid oral evidence led before 
Tribunal parties have also relied upon several documents 
proved m course of the hearing. Reliance has also been plac- 
ed by the parties on the evidence both oral and documentary 
recorded in course of the domestic enquiry including the 
report of the enquiry officer. 


6. It is an admitted fact that the three concerned work- 
men were working as underground munshis in the main dip 
10 Seam of Bhulanbararee Colliery at the 7th level junction 
from 1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974. rt is also not dis- 
puted that the pick coal was being raised during the afore- 
said period in the main dip at 10 Scam. It is admitted that 
the duties of the three concerned workmen who were oil 
underg ounj munshis at the relevant time were to submit 
reports regarding tubs of coal raised by each miner from 
the main dip 10 Seam. The report q submitted by the munshis 
are in writing and signed by them, They show the places 
from where pick coal is raised and put into tubs and also 
the names of the mineis who have raised the coal and put 
the same into tubs. The report, therefore, shows the number 
of tubs filled with coal by each miner. On the basri of this 
report bill is prepared for all the miners in the Bill Section. 
The Bill so prepared after countersignatures of the Colliery 
Manager as well as of the Assistant Colliery Manager is sent 
to the payment section where order for payment is passed 
and payments are made to the miners individually on pro- 
duction of their Identity cards In the presence of an officer 
of the colliery. It, therefore, follows that to rope an under- 
ground munshi in a charge of mis-appropriation of company's 
money on the basis of alleged over-reporting by him it has 
to be shown that there Is connivance between the munshi 
and miners and that out of the money received by the miners 
a portion is shared bv the munshi. The chargesheets Exts. 
M-1 to M-3 dated 27th June, 1974 show that the allegations 
in all of them are the same. Th e charges levelled against 
the three concerned workmen as per the three chargesheets 
are that they mis-anpropriated company’s fund by alleged 
over- reporting while booking tuh s of coal raised by the 
mmers Shrr G. Prasad learned counsel for the management 
fairly concedes that there is no evidence to show that there 
was connivance between the three concerned workmen and 
the mfneis in respect of whom there was over- reporting as 



1833 


[*mr II— ’ w* 3 (ii)] ^ 


per the report of the concerned woikmen. Mr. Prasad also 
concedes that the management has not been able to show 
that in the money paid to the miners on the false report of 
the three munshis which showed over-repoitimg the three 
concerned workmen had any share. Such being the position 
theie is no escape fiom the conclusion that the charge of 
misappropriation levelled against the thiee concerned work- 
men is without any evidence and therefore it must be held 
not to have been proved. In spite of these concessions by 
Mr, Prasad it is argued by him that even if a case of mis- 
appropriation is said not to have been established still the 
act of three workmen amounts to dishonesty in connection 
with company's business and that such an act is a misconduct 
under Rule 21(1 )(a) of the Standing Orders. This contention 
of Mr. Prasad is well founded. It cannot be denied that the 
report submitted by a munshi is a very important document 
on the basis of which payments arc made to the miners. This 
report shows number of tubs filled bv each miner with coal. 
The miner is paid on the basis of tubs filled by him as per 
the report. If really a miner has only filled two tubs with 
coal but the report showed that he has filled 5 tubs of coal 
payment has to be made to the miner for 5 tubs of coal. 
Therefore by making a false report like this a munshi Is 
certainly to be held to have acted dishonestly in respect of 
company’s business. So if sharing of money by a munshi 
with a miner is not proved still then a munshi will be held 
to have committed a misconduct having acted dishonestly 
in respect of company’s business. The charge sheet against 
the three concerned workmen is that in the main dip 10 Seam 
between 1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974 the total progress 
was only 15 ft., that according to dimension of drivage with 
a length of 15 ft. the total quantity of coal which could 
have been raised from a space with aforesaid dimention 
could only be 52.5 tubs, and that from the said space with 
the said dimention the three munshis had falsely booked 181 
tubs of coal. Tn the charge against tJ. S. Tiwary it has been 
$aid that out of 181 tubs of coal he as per his report had 
booked 63 tubs of pick coal. In the charge against Jalim 
Ram it is said that out of 181 tubs of coal he as per his 
report had booked 65 tubs of pick coal. In the charge against 
G. P. Choudhury it has been said that out of 181 tubs of 
coal he had booked 53 tubs of pick coal. So each individual 
charge against each of the concerned three workmen shows 
that each of the three concerned workmen had booked more 
than 52.5 tubs of pick coal in his report whereas according 
to the management from the scape with the aforesaid dimen- 
tion as given in the charge only 52.5 tubs of cnaJ would 
have been raised between 1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974. 
The union does not dispute these reports. On the other hand 
the concerned workmen admitted that they had reported as 
indicated in their reports. If the management’s case that 
from a space with a dimention as given in the chargesheet 
only 52.5 of pick coal could have been raised between 
1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974 is accepted it must be 
held that there was oven-reporting by the three concerned 
workmen. After careful scrutiny of evidence led on behalf 
of the management and after hearing both parties at length 
I find that the management's stand that from a space with 
the dimention as given in the chargesheet oply 52.5 ttfbs of 
pick coal could be raised within the relevant period is not 
seriously challenged. On the othen hand the union tries to 
show that under the orders of the Asstt. Colliery Manager 
and the Overman Tncharge the three concerned munshis were 
directed to book tubs of coal not only raised from the main 
dip 10 Seam but fiOm other places, namely, North Air Cross- 
ing, North Rise and South Rise. An attempt has also been 
made by the union to show that there had been a roof fall 
at the relevant place few days before 1st April, 19T4 - that 
as a result of the fall some coal had fallen down at the rele- 
vant place and that that coal which had fallen down was also 
carried alongwith pick coal raised by the miners from the 
main dip 10 Seam and put into tubs and that therefoie the 
number of tubs reported in the reports was more than what 
it should have been. It is thus very clear that the stand of 
the management which has been consistent throughout that 
52.5 tubs of coal would only have been raised from the 
drivage with a length of 15 ft. in main dip 10 Seam bet- 
ween 1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974 is accepted by the 
union as it does not say that mo f ie number of tubs of coal 
could have been raised as stated by the management from the 
place in question. After accepting the management’s stand 
the union comes forward with a case that \alongwith the 
tubs of coal that were raised from the relevant place during 
the period mentioned in the chargesheet, some other coal 
from some othen places as mentioned abov P had also been 
raised. Therefore, the only question that falls for determination

is as to whether coal from main dip 10 Seam which had only 
progressed upto 15 ft. was only raised between 1st April, 
1974 and 4th May, 1974 or along with that coal, coal from 
othe.' places had also been raised raising the total number of 
tubs of coal booked to 181 tubs. 

The chargesheels E\ts. M-1 to M-3 dated 27th June, 1974 
specifically say that during the period from 1st April, 1974 to' 
4th May, 1974 only 52.5 tubs of coal would have been 
raised from the main dip 10 Seam with a drivage of 15 ft. 
in length and that the concerned workmen during the same 
period as per their itportg had booked 181 tubs of coal 
showing that the same had been raised from the very same 
place from which according to chargesheets only 52.5 tubs 
of coal could have been raised. Tn the explanation submitted 
by the three concerned workmen Exts. M-4 to M-6 dated 
12th July, 1974 it has been said that it might be that the 
prog -ess of main dip of 10 Seam 7th level junction was 15 ft, 
only between 1st April, 1974 to 4th May, 1974, But accord- 
ing to the explanation because there was water trouble and 
tbe face could not be properly driven out due to pumping 
trouble, the concerned workmen were instructed by the 
Asslt. Collieiy Manager and Overman to engage miners 
of main dip in North Air Crossing, North Rise and South 
Rise and to book tubs of coal raised by the miners from 
those places as pick coal from main dip 10 Seam and that 
in pursuance to such instruction the tubs of coal the miners 
loaded from outside the main dip were also booked as pick 
coal in the name of main dip 10 Seam. In course of hearing 
of the case before the Tribunal a suggestion was made to one 
of the management's witnesses, namely, MW-2 that there was 
a roof fall at the relevant place shortly before 1st April, 
1974 and that as a result of the fall some coal came down 
from the roof which was also loaded along with the pick coal 
raised from main dip 10 Scam by the loaders and booked 
as pick coal from main dip only. Thus the story given in the 
explanation by the concerned workmen and the suggestion 
made to MW-2 in course of hearing go to show that tbe 
union is attempting to make out a case that along with pick 
coal from main dip 10 Scam the loaders not only raised 
coal from other places as mentioned in the explanations but 
also the coal which came down as a result of icof fall and 
that all the coal thm raised by the loaders in the tubs was 
booked as pick coal in the main dip 10 Seam raising the 
number of tubs from 52.5 to 181. MW-2 while admitting 
in cross-examination about roof fall has clearly stated that 
the coal which had come down from the roof was loaded 
on Hazri basis and was not mixed with pick coal raised from 
main dip 10 Seam. It is, however, argued by Mr. S. Bose 
appearing for the union that if thdre was truth in the version 
of MW-2 the management would have done well to produce 
papers to show that as a matter of fact coal which had fallen 
from the roof was raised as deposed to by MW-2. Non- 
production of documents by the management to show that 
the coal that had fallen from the roof was raised on Hazri 
basis will not affect the management's case in tny way 
because the plea of roof fall is completely absent from the 
explanation submitted by the concerned workmen Exts. 
M-4 to M-6. Tf as a matter of fact the union was serious 
about this plea it could have examined the loaders themsel- 
ves who could have said that they raised coal that had fallen 
from the roof along with pick coal raised from main dip 10 
Scam and that the entire coal thus raised by them was booked 
as pick coal from main dip 10 Seam as shown in the reports 
of the munshis. There is no reason why the union did not 
examine the miners, Tn the absence of any material prima 
facie to show that coal raised from other sources was also 
booked with pick coal raised from main dip 10 Seam the 
management cannot be expected to lead negative evidence. 
WW-2 who has examined himself as a witness foP the union 
does not breath a wofd about roof fall or about coal raised 
from sources other than from main dip 10 Seam as per the 
instruction of Asstt. Colliery Manager, Overman and Mining 
Sirdar. In th e departmental enquiry the three concerned 
workmen have examined themselves. They do not breath a 
word about roof fall. They have only deposed in the enquiry 
that beyond their explanation they have nothing more to 
say. Tn this circumstance the story about roof fall cannot 
be accepted and it cannot be said as claimed by the union 
that the coal that had fallen from the roof was raised 
along with the coal from main dit> 10 Scam and the entire 
coal thus raised was booked as pick coal from the main dip 
10 Seam. Regarding the story as given in the explanations 
that under the instructions of the Asstt. ColUerv Manager 
and Overman coal raised from North Air Ciosslng, North Rise 
and South Rise was also booked as pick coal from Main 
Dip 10 Seam the evidence for the union is practically nil.

In the departmental enquiry as has been pointed out earlier

to pick coal from main dip 10 Seam as per the instruction 
of the officers of the management. Even befoie the Tribunal 
WW-2 one of the concerned workmen examined for the union 
does not assert in his evidence that coal raised from other 
sources was booked as pick coal from main dip 10 Seam us 
per the instruction of the Asstt. Colliery Manacer and Over-

in support of this story. MW-2 the Asstt Colliery Manager 
when asked in cross-examination about raising of coal from 
North Air Crossing from North Rise and from South Rise 
has emphatically denied the fact. In other wards this witness 
who is a responsible officer of the management has stated 
that no coal raises from any other source was booked as 
pick coal from mam dip 10 Seam and that there was ever 
any such instruction. The union has not chosen to examine 
any of the miners or the Overman or the Mining Sirdar who 
was on duty at the relevant place at the relevant time, to 
prove the union’s case that coal raised from other sources 
was also booked as pick coal from main dip 10 Seam accord- 
ing to instruction of the management. True it may be said 
that none of the miners and none of the personnels of the 
technical supervisory staff would dare come to depose against 
the management and in favour of the union. Still then a 
finding regarding the plea taken by the concerned workmen 
bag to be arrived at on the basis of evidence. When there is 
no evidence in support of the pica of the union the finding 
must be against it. It is argued by Mr. Bose for the union 
that fn view of the specific plea taken in the explanations 
E xta. M-4 to M-6 it was incumbant on the management to 
examine the concerned miners, the overman and the con- 
cerned mining sirdar either in the domestic enquiry or before 
the Tribunal. As has been noticed earlier it is the case of the 
Union from the very beginning that under the instruction of 
the Asst. Colliery Manager and Overman, the concerned 
workmen booked coal raised from other places as pick coal 
from main dip 10 Seam. The management has examined one 
of the two persons mentioned in the explanation, namely, 
Asstt. ColUerv Manager as MW-2. He does not support the 
plea of the concerned workmen given in their explanations 
and emphatically denies the suggestion that no coal raised fiom 
other sources was booked as pick coal from main dip 10 
Scam. To examine the overman would have been duplication 
of evidence for the management. Certainly an Asstt. Colliery 
Manager is a more responsible person than an overman. So, 
thercftie, when the management has examined the Asstt. 
Colliery Manager it could not be blamed for non-examination 
of the overman, mining sirdar and the miners. It is, how 
ever, argued by Mr. Bose for the union that overman and 
mining sirdar belonging to Technical Supervisory staff were 
all along present to supervise raising of coal by the miners 
when pick coal was being raised from main dip 10 Seam 
during the relevant period whereas the Asstt. Colliery Manager 
was only supervising the work by going on inspection from time 
to time. Therefore the alleged over-reporting by the concerned 
workman must have been to their knowledge. So the over- 
man and mining sirdar would be more competent persons 
to testify about the falsify of union's case. When these two 
pei^ons have not been* examined the management must be 
held guilty of suppression of material evidence. According 
to Mr. Bose the miners who were engaged in loading would 
also be proper persons to depose about over-reporting and 
so non-examination of these persons by the management leaves 
a lacuna in management's case which must fail on that 
account. It has already been noticed that Asstt. Collieiy 
Manager has already been examined by the management as 
MW2 and he has emphatically denied the case madeout by the 
concerned workmen in their explanation, True Asstt, Colliery 
Manager is a person who does not remain present always 
when raising takes place, and therefore it may not be possi- 
ble for him to notice the alleged over-reporting at the place 
of occurrence itself or while passing the munshi reports to 
the Bill Sec. But so far as overman, mining sirdar and 
miners are concerned the v are more likely to notice the 
alleged over-reporting' at the site itself. Assuming that they 
had knowledge about the over-reporting and did not dis- 
close the same to the concerned authorities -mmediately the 
only inference that can be drawn is that they me either 
abetors of the offence committed by the concerned workmen 
or arc accomplices with the concerned workmen. That 
being so their evidence either befote the Enquiry Officer or 
before the Tribunal would not have helped much because 


that would not be independent evidence. So non -examination 
of these persons by the management is not fatal for the 
maagoment inasmuch as it has chosen to examine a more 
responsible person who was supervising the work at the 
relevant time. 

It is then argued by Mi. Bose that the field book which 
has been produced by the management both in the domestic 
enquiry as well as before the Tribunal is not countersigned by 
any officer in authority and therefore the same should not be 
taken into consideration. In my view this argument of 
Mr. Bose has no substance. The field book only shows the 
actual drivage at the relevant time in the main dip 10 Seam. 
It has nothing to do with actual quantity of coal raised. 
So far as drivage in the main dip 10 Seam is concerned I 
have already said that there is no dispute between parties. 
It is admitted by implication as per the explanation given by 
the concerned workman that the lenght of the drivage as 
mentioned in the chargesheet is not only correct but the 
quantity of coal that could have been raised from a length, 
of 15 ft. in the main dip 10 Seam between 1st April, 1974 
to 4th May, 1974 as given in the chargesheet is also correct 
That being so the criticism which Mr. Bose makes on the 
Field Book is of no assistance to the union. 

It is further afgued by Mr. Bose that the allegation In 
the chargesheet that the concerned workmen misappropriated 
is not proved. He points out the evidence led by the union 
that a munshi’s report after it is prepared and signed bv the 
munshi goes to Asstt. Colliery Manager's office who there- 
after sends it to the Bill Clerk where bill is prepared in the 
name of individual miners on the basis of the munshi’s re- 
port. After the bill is prepared the same is countersigned by 
the manager and orders are passed for payment. After this 
the bill goes to the payment section where payments are 
made to the individual miners in the presence of an officer. 
That being the position unless connivance is established bet- 
ween the miners and the concerned munshis to show that 
the miners after receiving payment under the bills parted 
with a portion thereof in favour of the munshis it Is not 
possible to say that the munshis committed any misappro- 
priation. This argument of Mr. Bose is very sound. I entirely 
agree with him that a case of misappropriation has not been 
established but the concerned workmen have been chaiged 
under para 21(1 )(a) of the Standing Orders which says that 
theft, fraud or dishonesty in connection with the employers 
business or property are misconducts. Although charge men- 
tions misappropriation the provision contained in para 
21(l)(a) of the Standing Orders does not refer to it. The 
concerned workmen were specifically told that they were 
charged for misconduct as mentioned in this provision. There- 
fore it has to be seen whether the concerned woikmen com- 
mitted dishonesty in respect of management's business and 
property. Payments are made to each minor for ihe num- 
bers of tubs of coal booked in his name in the munshi report. 
Therefore if a munshi over-reports and on the basis of over- 
reporting payment is made it must be held that the munshi 
in his act of over-reporting commits dishonesty in respect of 
management’s business and property. In the present case It 
has been noticed that even though number of tubs of pick 
coal that could have been booked In main dip 10 Scam 
between the given period is only 52.5 the concerned munshis 
have reported 181 tubs. Each one of them has reported 
more than 52.5 tubs, I have already negatived the plea of 
the union that tubs of coal booked in the concerned munshis’ 
reports do not include coal raised from other sources as per 
the case of the union. Therefore conclusion is irresistible 
concerned munshis have deliberately over-reported and have 
thereby acred dishonestly while dealing with company’s busi- 
ness and property. It Is argued by Mr, Bose that a munshi 
only sits at one place underground and books the number 
of tubs of coal brought to him by different mineis. The 
munshi is not required to see wherefrom the coal is brought, 
According to Mr, Bose it is the duty of the overman and 
the mining sirdar who are at the spot to see that the miners 
engaged to raise coal from a particular place raise the same 
from that place only and do not raise coal from any other 
place. That being ro a munshi could not be held responsible 
for over-reporting because he has no knowledge as to where- 
from the coal is brought to him. This position is not correct. 
Even though the overman and the mining sirdar as personnel 
belonging to technical supervisor staff look after the raising 
it cannot be said that it is not the duty of the munshi to see 
the place wherefrom the coal is brought. As a matter of 
fact the technical supervisor staff allot gangs for raising coal 
from different sites to the knowledge of munshis. In other

woids the munshis are told by the technical supervisory staff 
as to from what place the miners of each gang me to raise

knowledge of oi at the connivance of the miners, overman 
and mining sirdar But that would not cxDneiatc the three

coal is to be laised and to take sufficient safety measures.

when he wrongly makes an entry in this column An argu-

as I have already said the location and dimention of the 
place wherefrom coal was raised not being disputed, non- 
production of surveyor’s report m the enqimy will not affect 
the merit of the case against the management A surveyor’s 
report wil] only show the extent of drivage This is not dis- 
puted as I have already pointed out Therefore, even accept- 
ing the contention of Mr Bose that suiveyor's report was 
not produced in the enquiry it cannot be said that the man- 
agement has not proved its case against the concerned work- 
men Merely because other officers namely, the mining sirdar 
and the overman as contended by Mr Bose either deliberately 
ovei looked the over-reporting or had any hand m the over- 
reporting that would not be a circumstance to exonerate the 
authors of the report, namely, the three concerned workmen. 
Assuming for the time being that other officers of the man- 
agement aic also responsible foi over-reporting committed 
by three concerned workmen that would not be an extenuat- 
ing circumstance m favour of the concerned workmen The 
misconduct for which the three concerned workmen have 
been charged is of a very senous nature and certainly calls 
for punishment of severe type l c dismissal Tn my view the 
three concerned workmen have been lightly found guilty 
and have been rightly punished and the impugned order of 
punishment do not call for interference 

For the reasons stated above T hold that the misconduct 
for which the thiec concerned workmen have been charged has 
been fully proved against them and the action taken by the 
management against the fhree concerned workmen dismissing 
theft from service with effect from 21st April, 1975 is justi- 
fied The reference is answered accordingly In the circum- 
stance there will be no order for cost 

B K. RAY, Presiding Officer 
[No L-20012/158/75-D JII(A)1

For the Workman — Shn B. N Sharma, Joint General

INDUSTRY Coal STATE : Bihar, 

Dated, ihe 7th May, 1981 
AWARD 

The Government of India in the Ministry of Labour In 
exercise of the poweis conferred on them U/a I0(l)(d) of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 14 of 1947 have referred the 
following dispute to this Tubunal for adjudication by their 
Order No L 200 12/ 161/77 -D.III(A) dated the 2lst February. 
1978 

SCHEDULE 

"Whether the action of the management of Dikwadih 
Collieiy of M/s Tata Iron & Steel Co. Ltd. P.O. 

Jamadoba Dhanbad) m dismissing Md. Kfimal 
Clipman /Banksman with effect from 12th July, 1976 
is justified ? If not, to what relief is the said work- 
man entitled ?” 

2 The case of the workman is that he was In the perma- 
nent employment of the company as a Clipman and had 
worked for more than 14 years and the duties and responsi- 
bilities performed by him were that of a Clipman, The job 
description of a Clipman has been defined in the Central 
Wage Board for Coal Mining Industry. 

3 It is stated that the concerned woikman wa s charge- 
sheeted on 5th December, 1975 alleging that on 3rd Decem- 
ber 1975 he was deputed to work as a Banksrmm in A shift 
at 9 Pit top from 8 a m. to 4 p m It was alleged that during 
the course of his duty at about 10 15 am the south side 
cage had just come up at the Kep’s level and the concerned 
workman carelessly opened the fencing gate of the other side 
viz north side and pushed a tub loaded with cement and 
sand which fell down id the shaft and hit the bottom cage at 
the depth of about 450 ft as a result of which the bottom 
cage and the guide rope were badly damaged which ulti- 
mately led to the stoppage of pit winding and production for 
two shifts 

4 The concerned workman denied the charge and submitted 
that as per Regulation 84(1) of the Coal Mines Regulations, 
1957 it is clean that the actual at the top of the shaft shall be 
self operating but the actual fact was that the gate was not 
self operating and the management is guilty of the violation 
of the said regulation According to him it was only due 
to the rolling down of the tubs in question which fell down 
in the shaft It is also stated that the enquiry was not held 
in accordance with the principles of natural justice and be 
has been dismissed illegally The workman has also submitted 
that the duty of a Bankman was thrust upon him against his 
wishes and the management is entirely responsible for the 
said mishap He challenged the fairness of the enquiry In 
various other ways and it i s submitted that he be reinstated 
with full back wages 

5 The case of the management however is that concerned 
workman had been working as Banksman at Digwadih Col- 
liery and on the date of occurrence he was in A shift duty 
at 9 Pit top fiom 8 am to 4 pm It i s stated that during 
the com se of his duLy at about 10 15 am. the south side 
cage had just come up at the Kep’s level and the concerned 
workman carelessly opened the fencing gate of the north 
side and pushed a tub loaded with cement and sand which 



1836 


THE GAZETTE OF FmDIA : JUNE 6, 1981/JYAIStHA 16, 1903 [Fart II— She, 3(H)] 


fell down in the shaft and hit the bottom cage at the depth 
of about 450 ft. as a result of which the bottom cage and 
the guide rope were badly damaged leading to the stoppage 
of the pit winding and production for two shifts. It is stated 
that for the above misconduct a chargesheet was issued 
against the concerned workman to which he submitted a 
reply, A departmental enquiry was conducted in presence 
of the concerned workman, Md. Kamal and he was given 
full opportunity to cross-examine the management's witnesses 
and also adduced his evidence to defend himself. In the 
departmental enquiry, however, the charges were fully proved 
against him and the competent authority agreeing with the 
findings of the Enquiry Officer dismissed the concerned work’ 
man with effect from 12th July, 1976. It la submitted that 
the job of Banksman is veiy important and the concerned 
workman committed a very serious misconduct in us much 
as due to hTs negligence accident would have happened. It is 
also stated that Md. Kamal had submitted his grievance 
through the Grievance Procedure Machinery functioning at 
the colliery and the Gioup Joint Grievance Committee con- 
sisting of both management and workman representatives dis- 
cussed the case of Md. Kamal and an unanimous decision 
was taken against him and it was held that the dismissal was 
justified and no injustice had been done to him. 

6. In the rejoinder the management has denied that the 
workman of Banksman had been thrust upon the concerned 
workman against his will and according to the management 
the concerned workman was doing the job of Banksman 
since lorfg. 

7. The preliminary issue as to whether the enqtdiy was 
just and proper was heard by my prcdecesior-in-ollicc who 
by his order dated 15th September, 1980 held that the en- 
quiry was just and proper and there was no denial of natural 
justice. 

8. The only point now for consideration is as to whether 
the action of the management in dismissing the concerned 
workman from 12th July, 1976 is justified and if not what 
relief he is entitled to, 

9. For the aforesaid decision this Court has to look Into 
the enquiry proceedings and N Ce as to whether there was 
sufficient evidence before the management to hold the con- 
cerned workman guilty of the charge. 

10. The management has examined MW-1 Sri I,. Prasad, 
Astflt. Manager who has stated that the departmental enquiry 
was held against the concerned workman by Sri Binay Kr. 
Verma, Personnel Officer of the company. As the Enquiry 
Officer had left his service and his whereabouts were not 
known hence he could not be examined. This witness how^ 
ever was the Presiding Officer duiing the enquiry. The work- 
man has examined himself as WW-1 and hag denied the charge. 
Ext. M*1 is the chargesheet dated 3rd December, 1975 issued 
against the workman which reads as follow! : 

“Charge Sheet No. 100. 

Name Sri Md. Kamal. T. No. 37160, 

Designation Ag. Banksman (Clipman) 

Section Dikwadih Colliery. 

You are hereby asked to show cause why discip- 
lihaiy action should not be taken against you under 
Clause 19(6) & 19(19) to be read with Reg. 190 of 
Coal Mines Regulations 1957 of the Standing Orders 
for the following misconduct. 

You were deputed to work as Banksman in ‘A’ 
shift of 3rd December, 1975 at 9 Pit top. 

At about 10.15 a.m. the South cage had just come 
up at the keps level. You carelessly opened the 
empty side fencing gate of the North cage portion 
and pushed down a tub loaded with cement and 
sand which fell down into the shaft and hit the 
bottom cage at a depth of about 530 ft. It badly 
damaged the cage and guide rope, and resulted in 
stoppage of pit winding and pi od action for two 
shifts. This is a very serious act of negligence on 
your part. 

Also you have violated the Section 190 of Coal 
Mines Regulations 1957. 

You are allowed 72 hours from the date of re- 
ceipt here of two give your explanation. 


Any representation that you may make in this 
connection will be taken into consideration before 
passing orders. 

Dated 5-12-75 

Sd /- Manager 
Digwadih Colliciy." 

The workman vide Ext. M-l/A denied the charge and threw 
the lesponsibility on the management saying that the gate 
of the shaft was not self operating. Exts. M-3 & M-4 arc 
the documents to show that time was extended during the 
enquiry. Ext. M-5 is the dismissal letter! dated 3rd July, 1976 
dismissing the concerned workman with effect from 12th July, 
1976. Ext. M-7 is the enquiry proceeding. From a perusal 
of the enquiry proceeding it will appear that on behalf of the 
management Sn Lalan Prasad, Safety Officer, Sri P. N. Singh, 
Manager, Rajmangal Singh, Trammer and Sri Bijoy Kumar 
Verma, Asstt. Manager were examined. Md, Kamal the con- 
cerned workman has examined himself as D, W. Sri L. Prasad 
is the Safety Officer and he wag also the Piesenting Officer 
during the enquiry. He has stated that on the date and time 
of occurrence he was in his chamber at pit top office and 
that at about 10.15 a.m. he got report that one tub loaded 
with cement and sand had fallen down in Pit No. 9 from 
the empty side and so he rushed to the spot and found 
several peisons there. He enquired into the matter and before 
him the concerned woikman was looking very nervofls and 
he admitted his guilt. The concerned workman was present 
during hig examination but he declined to cross-examine him. 
Witness No. 2 is Sri P, N. Singh, the Manager who also sent 
to the place of occurrence when he was informed by Raj- 
mangal Singh, Trammer. He has also stated about the fall- 
ing of the tub and the damage caused by it. His cross- 
examination has also been declined. The next witness is 
RajmangaJ Singh, Trammel i who is an eye witness to the 
incident. He has given the details and has staled that the 
concerned workman went towards the empty side arid opened 
the northern gale where there were 7 tubs and the' first tub 
was full of sand and cement. He hag further stated that the 
concerned workman without asking or without getting any 
signal went towards the empty side and opened the sprag of 
the first tub and pushed it to the northern side. The tub went 
straight inside the shaft and rested at pit bottom at 350 ft 
belaw. According to him it was due to gross negligence of 
the concerned woikman that this incident occurred. The last 
witness is Sri Bijay Kumar Verma, Assistant Manager. 
According to him he came to the pit top and after giving 
signal to be allowed to go down he went down from the 
northern cage. He has also given the other details, 

11. From the evidence of the above witness it will appear 
that the incident in question was caused due to the negli- 
gence of the concerned workman as he without taking any 
precaution allowed the tub to go down from the northern 
cage. 

12. Though the concerned workman in his written state- 
ment ha s stated that the work of Banksman had been thrust 
upon him against his will, but has not come to support this 
fact in his evidence. Rather, it is clear from the evidence on 
record that he was working as a Banksman since long. The 
duties of a Banksman has been defined U/S 52 of the Coal 
Mines Regulation, 1957. Any negligence of this section 
amounts to gross misconduct under the Standing Order of 
the company which is on the record. From the evidence ad- 
duced during the enquiry proceeding it is clear that the 
incident in question was caused due to negligence of the 
concerned workman and the charges were well proved against 
him. The Enquiry Officer in his report Ext. M-8 has given 
cogent reasons for holding the concerned workman guilty of 
the charge after discussing the entire evidence adduced before 
him and it cannot be said that there was no cogent evidence 
against the concerned workman to hold him guilty. 

13. Considering the evidence and record of the case, I hold 
that the chaiges against the concerned workman were well 
proved and fiis dismissal is justified. 

14. The woikman in the circumstances is not entitled to 
any relief. 

15. r give my award accordingly. 

J. N. SINGH, Presiding Officer

S.O. 1686.— In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Ccential Government 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govern- 
ment Industrial Tribunal, Madras, in the industrial dispute bet- 
ween the employeis in relation to the management of State 
Bank of India and their workman, which was received by tjie 
Central Government on the 12th May, 1*981. 

BEFORE THIRU T. SUDARSANAN DANIEL, E.A., B.L., 

PRESIDING OFFICER, INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL. 

MADRAS 

(Constituted by the Government of India) 

Monday, 4th May, 1981 

Industrial Dispute No. 78 of 1980. 

(In the matter of the dispute for adjudication under Sec- 
tion 10(1) (d) of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 
between the workmen and the Management of State 
Bank of India, Madras.] 

BETWEEN 

Thiru S. Stuitharmkrishnan, No. 18. South Street Sarukkah 
P. O. Kabisthalam-6 14203, Thanjavar District Tamil 
Nadu. 

AND 

The Chief General Manager, Stale Bunk of India,, Madras 
LHO, No, 21, Madras-1. 

Reference i 

Order No. L-12012U81|79-D.II.A., dated 7th October, 
1980 of the Ministry of Labour. Government of 
India. 

This dispute coming on for final hearing on Thursday, the 
5th day of March, 1981, upon perusing the reference, claim 
and courmter statements and all other material papers on re 
cord and upon hearing the arguments of Thiru K. Chundru 
for Thiruvalargal Row and Reddy and K. Chandru, Advocate 
for the woikman and of Thiru M. Muhalingam, Advocate 
appearing lor the Management and this dispute having stood 
over till this day for consideration, this Tribunal made the 
following. 

AWARD 

This is an Industrial Dispute between the workmen and the 
Management of State Baank of India, Madras referred to this 
Tribunal for adjudication under Section 10(1) (d) of the 
Industrial Dispute Act, 1947 by the Government of India in 
Older No. 12G12[18l|79-D.lLA. 3 dated 7th October, 1980 
of the Ministry of Labour, in respect of the following issue : 

Whether the action of the management of State Bank of 
India, Madras in terminating abruptly the sendees 
of Shri Samhanakrishnan temporary clerk 011 15 2-74 
is justified V If not, to what relief is the workman 
concerned entitled 7 

(2) Facts leading upto this dispute are as folio as : The 
Management is Slate Bank of India represented by the Chief 
Gencwd Manager, Madias 1 HO, No. 21, Madrasi, Tamil 
Nadu. The reference made by the Government of India re- 
lates to the action of the Management in having terminated 
the services of Thiru S. Santhanakrishnan on 16-2-197-1. The 
claimant Thiru S. Santhanakrishnan was directed to appear 
by the Branch Manager of Chongam Branch of the Manage- 
ment by order dated 17-5-1973 Ex. W-l for interview for 
being appointed as a temporary clerk. On 22nd May. 1973 
he was appointed ns a temporary clerk of Chongam Branch 
for a peiiod of 15 days from 22nd May, 1973 to 5lh June, 
1971 under Fx W-2 On 16-2-1974, the Branch Manager 
issued an older Fx W-3 stating that as the Claimant Thiru 
S. Santhanakrishnan have had completed 265 dcivs of service 
in their Branch, his services are no lonizei required. Thus it 
is apparent that the claimant Thiru S> Santhinakrishnan was 
engaged as temporary employee by the Management at 
Chongam Branch during the period from 22-5-1973 to 16-2-74. 



1838 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JUNE 6, 1981/JYA1STHA 16, 19#3 [Part JI^S £ c. 3(ii)] 


Admittedly, ho has completed 265 days of service as tempo- 
iary clerk. The ttand ot the Manafieemeent 13 that the Clai- 
mant Ihiru S. Santhanakrishnan had not completed 240 days 
of service during a period of 12 calendar months and hence 
he is not entitlcJ to the benefits of Chapter V-A of the In- 
dustrial Disputes Act, 1947. The Claimant Thiru S Santhana- 
krishnan had joined the Management on 22-5-1973 and his 
services were teiminated on 16-2-1974. Therefore it is clear 
that lrom May, 1973 till February, 1974 during a period of 
10 months he has put in 265 days of service as a temporary 
clerk as is e^ ident from the order of the Management Ex. 
W- 3. In 57 F.J.R, Page 67 (Surendra Kumar Veima and 
others vs. Central Government Industrial Tribunal-cum-Laboui 
Court, New Delhi* and (mother) the Supreme Court has poin- 
ted out that as per amendment of Section 25-B(2) of the 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, it is designed to provide that 
a workman who has actually worked under an employer tor 
not less than 240 days during a period of 12 months shall be 
deemed to have been in continuous service for a period of 
one year and that it is no longer necessary that he should be 
In employment during the preceding period of twelve calendar 
months in order to qualify within the terms of Section 25-B 
and it is sufficient if he has actually worked for not less than 
240 days in a period of 12 months. Therefore, the retrench- 
ment of Claimant Thiru S. Santhanakrishnan would well come 
within the Hmbit of Section 25-B of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, 1 947, It is not gain-said that neither the notice contemp- 
lated under Section 25F (a) nor any compensation contemp- 
lated under Section 25F(b) of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 had been paid to the Claimant Thiru S. Santhanakrishnan 
at the time of retrenchment on 16-2-1974. In the ciicum- 
stances* the termination of the Claimant Thiru S. Santhana- 
krishnan who had worked for 265 days during the period of 
one year without complying with the provisions of Section 
25-F of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 is invalid and in- 
operative. Thai has been laid down by the Supreme Court 
in 197b — I-L.L.L Page 478 (State Bank of India vs. N. Sunda- 
ramonev) where It F pointed out that “if the workman swims 
into the harbour of Section 25F, he cannot be retrenched 
without payment at the time of retrenchment compensation 
as prescribed therein read with Section 25B(2).” 


(3) That leads me to the next question as to the relief 
that should be awarded to th e Claimant Thiru S. Santhnna- 
krishnan, Learned counsel for the workman Thiru Chandru 
points out that in the absence of any special circumstances 
pleaded and proved by the Management, the normal relief of 
reinstatement with full back wages must be ordered. Support 
tor this position is sought to be had from the latest decision 
of the Supreme Court in 57 F.J.R. Page 67 (Surendra Kumar 
Verma and others vs. Central Government Industrial Tribunal- 
cum-Labour Court, New Delhi and another). Where It has 
been pointed out that ordinarily, a workman who has been 
retrenched in conti uvention of the law is entitled to rein- 
statement with full back wages and that principle yields only 
where the justice of the case in the light of exceptional cir- 
cumstances makes it impossible or wholly inequitable vis. a 
vis the employer and workmen to direct reinstatement with 
full back wages In exceptional cases there is a vestige of 
discretion left in the Court to make appropriate consequen- 
tial orders. Therefore, on the facts of the present case, it 
has to be seen whether there is any special impediment in the 
way of awarding the normal relief of reinstatement with full 
back wages. My answer would be in the affirmative 
Here are my reasons : In the first place, admittedly, the 
Claimant was employed by the Management only as a tem- 
porary clerk. In order to be confirmed as a regular clerk, the 
incumbent has to pass a necessary test held by the Manage- 
ment. In the present case, although the Claimant had last 
worked under the Management on 16-2-1974 yet in. 1977. 
Jie w r as permitted to apnear for a text. But unfortunately, failed 
to qualify himself. Therefore, even if he had been con- 
tinued in the employment from 16-2-1974 till 1977, yet he 
would not have been confirmed as a regular clerk because 
he has failed to pas c the required test. But learned counsel 
for the Claimant Thiru K, Chandru ooints out that even 
assuming that the Claimant ha 4 ? not come out successful in 
the test conducted for permanent absorptions, even then his 
temporary appointment must he terminated in accordance with 
law and he places reliance on the latest decision rf die Sup- 
reme CoiiTt umoiled in 1980-H-T T T. Pave 72 fSanthosh 
Guntn vs Slate Bank of Patiala 1. Hnwbeit it muri be noted 
that the ^rvices of Claimant had not been terminated because 
of his f ail n re to pas-' the reauired test. Therefore this deci- 
sion while adorning that the termination of the Claimant on 


16-2-19/4 is patently invalid and inoperative for the non- 
compliance of provision of Section 25-F ot the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947, it would not in any way further the case 
of the Claimant to any extent. It the Claimant had been 
continued in service even after 16-2-1974 and his services 
had been terminated consequent on his failuie to pass a 
rcquiied ie^t, even then, he cannot be retrenched by the 
Management without complying with the conditions laid 
down in Section 25-F ot the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947. 
But on the facts of the present case, it is perfectly dear that 
if ieinstatement is ordered now it would mean that the Clai- 
mant must be deemed to be as a temporary employee from 
16-2-1974 till date fot over 7 years. Normally, a temporal y 
employee would never be considered to be a temporary for 
a period exceeding one year. In the circumstances, it would 
be most inexpedient to order actual reinstatement of Ihe Clai- 
mant Thiru S. Sanihakrishnan with effect from 16-2-1974. 

(4) As already pointed out, prior to the termination of the 
service.^ of the Claimant from 16-2-1974, no notice nor com- 
pensation was ofieied to him in direct violation of mandatory 
provision of Section 25-F of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947. 
As per paragraph 522(4) of the Sastry Award governing Ihe 
employees ot the Banks, an employee would be entitled to 14 
days salary in lieu of notice. Admittedly, even this 14 days 
salary in Jicu ot notice was not paid to the Claimant at the 
time of tei ruination of his services on 16-2-1974, However, at 
the intervention of the State Bank of India Staff Union, the 
Management has paio the Claimant 14 days salary in lieu of 
notice as contemplated in paragraph 522(4) of the Sastry 
Award. The stand of the Management is that the Claimant 
has accepted this payment without any demur in full and final 
settlement ot all his claims. It is w^ell settled that accep- 
tance of nolice pay would not bar a workman from challenge 
ing the validity of the retrenchment itself. There cannot be a 
waiver of any statutory rights. If authority is needed I would 
only refer to 1977-II-L.L.J. Page 137, where a Division Bench 
of our High Court had confirmed 50 F.J.R, Page 28 (R. 
Sankara ti vs. Additional Labour Court, Madras and another). 
To a similar efiect is also an earlier decision of our High Court 
reported in 37 F.J.R. Page 249 (Sree Rajcndra Mills Ltd., 
vs. Labour Court, Coimbatore and others), where it wag poin- 
ted out that the mandate under the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 is imperative and it imports an implied avoidance of any 
arranagement or settlement outside the provisions of riiat 
chapter between the employer and the workmen and hence 
it cannot be contended that an agreement de hors the pro- 
visions of the Industrial Disputes Act can be passed into ser- 
vice, in the face of the express provision of Industrial Dis- 
putes Act, 1947, Therefore, I am unable to accept the case 
of the Management that the Claimant has settled his claim 
against the Management of receipt of 14 days salary in lieu of 
notice as prescribed in paragraph 522(4) of the Sastry Award. 

(5) The services of the Claimant were terminated on 
16-2-1974, The contention of the Management is that the 
Claimant had not completed 240 day® of service during a 
period of 12 calendar months and hence he is not entitled to 
the benefit of Chapter V-A of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947. This position has been taken up by the Management 
apparently on the basis of an earlier decision of the Supreme 
Court in 1963 — 25 F.J.R. Pace 88 (Sur Enamel and Stamping 
Works (?) Ltd., vs. their workmen), However, in 57 F.J R. 
Page 67, the Supreme Court has pointed out that the aforesaid 
decision dees not apply to situation governed by the subse- 
quently amended Section 25-B of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 by the amendment of Act of 1964. Therefore, whatever 
may be the justification of the stand of the Management, it 
cannot be said as the law then stood in 1974 or 1977, the 
view of the Management was altogether unjustified. However, 
a shred of cloud that existed over the horizon w?h regard 
to the conditions for invoking Section 25-B of the Industrial 
Disnules Act. 1947 hag been finally dispersed by the Supreme 
Court in the latest decision reported in 57 F.J.R. Page 67. 
Regard being had to these peculiar circumstances, the atti- 
tude adopted by the Management towards Iho claim of 
the Claimant cannot be totally described as perverse or un- 
tenable. 

(6) Jt is nFo submitted by the Management that the claim 
of the Petitioner for ieinstatement has become stale ns he is 
gniltv of lathes in rairinp the Industrial Dispute five veers 
after the termination of bus temnorarv service. Of course, 
there cannot be anv valid bar of limitation to the cl dm But 
one has to look to the attending circumstances to find out 



1839 


£«pt 3(ii)] 


STITT ¥T WBT ; Tq 6 , 198l/sJ?*S 1 6/ 1903 


if there is any plausible explanation for his failure to raise 
an industrial dispute for live yeurs. I have already pointed 
out to the fact that at the intervention of the State Bank of 
India Staff Union, the Management has paid the Claimant 
14 days’ salary in lieu of notice as provided for in paragraph 
522(4) of the Sastry Award. Meanwhile, the Management 
had also permitted the Claimant to sit for test to qualify 
himself for the post of clerk, but Claimant failed to qualify 
himself in the Recruitment Test held on 18-12-1977. The 
Claimant moves the Regional Labour Commissioner (Central), 
Madras for the first time only on 18-1-1979 vide Ex, W-4, 
From Ex. W-4, it can be noted that the Bank has appraised 
the Claimant by a letter dated 9-3-1978 to the ellect that 
Claimant has not come out successful in the recruitment test 
held on 18-12-1977. Even after the receipt of such communi- 
cation, the Claimant has not moved his little finger to claim 
for reinstatement by the Management. That apart, from 
16-2-1974 till date several temporary candidates must have 
been duly confirmed as regular candidates and as T have al- 
ready indicated that if the reinstatement of the Claimant is 
ordered, bis status can only be that of a temporary candi- 
date. thus unsettling the claims of several others who had been 
made as permanent clerks. Therefore, although the right 
of the Claimant to raise the present dispute even pftcr live 
years is not barred by limitation, yet regard being had to 
the passage of time and several factors mentioned above it is 
not desirable or expedient that the reinstatement of the 
Claimant should be ordered. For the reasons listed by me. 
1 am unable to find that on the facts of the case, only rein- 
statement of the Claimant is wholly justifiable or equitable 

(7) That takes rne to the final consideration as to the 
actual relief that has to be gi anted to the Claimant. The ends 
ol justice can be met by ordering payment of compensation 
to the Claimant full wages on the basis of last drawn pay 
from 17-2-1974 till the date of the Award. 

(8) In the result, an Award Is passed holding that the Clab 
mant Thiru S. Santhanakrishnan is not entitled to be rein- 
stated but that he is entitled to compensation of wages on Ihe 
basis of last drawn fiom 17-2-1974 till the date of the Awaid. 
In the peculiar circumstances, I direct the parties to beai 
their respective costs. 

Dated, this 4th day of May, 1981. 

T. SUDARSANAM DANJEL, Presiding Officer

AWARD 

The Central Govt, as appropriate Govt, vide its order 
No. L-J2012I19I79-DJT.A dated the 30th June, 1979 icferrcd 
an Industiial Dispute to this Tribunal u/s 10 of the Indus-* 
trial Disputes Act, 1947 in the following terms ; 

'Whether the action of the management of Oriental Bank 
of Commerce Ltd,, New Delhi in appointing Sri 
Kulbhushan Jain as an Apprentice with effect from 
1-5-1971 after his 118 days satisfactory temporary 
service in the Bank as a Clerk is justified ? If not, 
to v-hat relief is the said woikmun entitled T 

2. After the i\;lerence was registered usual notices were sent 
to the parties. The workman appeared in person while re* 
piesentativc cf the Management appeared, A statement of 
claim was filed. Then a written statement was filed by the 
Bank. Then finally n replication was filed and the case was 
adjourned foi filing of documents to 6th December. 1979. 
Thereafter documents were filed by the parties. The cas# 
was fixed for 29th of September, 1980 for admission and 
denial. On which date non appeared for the workman while 
the counsel for the Management had appeared. Then the case 
was adjourned to 1st December, 1980 on which date again 
none appeared for the workman. The case was then ad- 
journed to 5th January* 1981 and again none appeared for the 
workman. Finally when even on 23-2-1981, the next dat# 
fixed none appeared for the workman, ex-part© ordered 
against the workman side and following Issues were framed: 

ISSUES : 

1. Whether the matter under reference does not quality 

as an I. D. ? 

2. Whether the workman side is estopped from raising 

this dispute ? 

3. As in order of reference. 

Thereafter cx-parte evidence of the Bank was recorded which 
consists of statement of Shri Surinder Narain Bhatnagar as 
MW I who has tendered his affidavit Ex. M.W. Ill apart 
from documents Fx. M|1 to Ex, M|8 in the evidence and 
thereafter mguments were heard. I have gone through the 
pleadings of the parties as also the evidence led by the Bank 
and after ghing my considered thought to the matter befoi# 
me I have come to the following findings : 

3. The contention of the workman is that the workman 
was employed as a clerk in Dehradun Branch of the Bank 
on 8th January, 1971 and he worked there till the end of 
May 1971 : that to utter surprise of the workman his ap- 
pointment vide communication dated 28-5-1971 was conver- 
ted into a case of fresh recruitment as a paid apprentice: that 
as the workman had been woiklng as a clerk and was per- 
forming the job against a permanent vacancy his fresh appoint- 
ment as an apprentice was irregulai; that on his raising ob- 
jection he was threatened that his services even as appren- 
tice would be terminated and therefore the workman could 



1840 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JUNE 6, 1981/JYAISTHA 16, 1903 [Part Tl— S ec. 3(iij] 


do iiothinft; that after he had worked for about six months 
he was appointed as a probationer and sub^eq ucntly he vva* 
Confumed lrom 5th May, 1972; that the Management had not 
taken into account 180 days rendered by the workman in tht 
service of the Hank which is illegal and hence he was entitled 
that temporary service for 180 days from 8-1-71 to 31-5-71 
und the period during such period from 8-1-71 to 31-5-71 
be accounted for the purposes of fixing his seniority and 
wages and he is entitled to other benefits, 

4. In reply the bank contended that no demand was raided 
upon the Bank and as such the matter did not qualify n* 
an Industrial Dispute and as such the reference was bad; 
that the workman was estopped from raising the dispute in 
as much as the matter regarding counting of temporal y sei- 
vice in respect of the workman’s staff already is settled vide 
settlement dated the 16th March, 1978 which is duly regis- 
tered settlement. The facts of the case are otherwdse not 
denied but it is alleged that the workman W'as not entitled 
to any relief what-so-ever. 

Issue No. 1 ; 

5. Frorn the perusal of the order of reference it would h* 
found that the mattei under reference is a collective dispute 
and is not covered by the provisions of Section 2-A of the 

I. D. Act. In view thereof it would follow that certainly it 
was essential that a formal dispute should have been raised 
and then and then only an Industrial Dispute could be said 
to have come into existence. There is nothing on record tc 
suggest even remotely that such a dispute was ever raised. 
In view thereof it would follow that the matter under reference 
does not qualify as an Industrial Dispute. From the perusal 
of para 2 of affidavit Ex. M.W, 111 it would be found that it 
is categorically stated that no such dispute was ever raised 
by the workman side with the Management and as such 
this issue is decided in favour of the Management. 

Issue No. 2 : 

6. The Management has filed on record extracts from 
settlement dated 16-3-1978 which have been exhibited as Mjl, 
From the perusal of Ex. M|I it would be found that tha 
case of the workman is not covered by it and the workman 
side therefore would be estopped from now challenging what 
was arrived at in settlement Ex. M[l. It was open to the 
workman side and the union to take up this matter and if 
it has ignored it and has arrived at different settlement this 
workman cannot now be said to be entitled to raise it 
afresh. The case of the workman Is not covered by item I 
and as such the woikman could not now claim the benefit 
of 180 days service rendered by him and the union is estopped 
from raising this issue. Reference may also be made to para 
3 of the affidavit which is categorical in this behalf and it 
proves the conlenlions of settlement Fx. M|L, In view 

all these facta this issue is also decided against the workman 
3lde. 

Issue No. 3 : 

7. From the perusal of para 4 it would be found that the 
workman was appointed as a temporaray clerk in leave arran- 
gement for the period from 8-1-71 to 31-5-71 vide appoint- 
ment letter Fx. M|2. That being the position this appoint- 
ment to fill a leave vacancy temporarily would not enable 
the workman to the benefit he now claims. The appointment 
as paid apprentice of the workman was made in pursuance of 
advertisement in the Newspaper vide letter dated 1 5-3-1971 
The workman also was advised to appear for written test 
dated 28-3-71 as a reference to para 6 of the affidavit would 
show, This was in pursuance to application Ex. Ml4 hv «' * 
workman. The wort man had in fact appeared for the written 
test and was found suitable and Was absorbed as a paid 
apprentice vide letter dated 28-3-71, copy Fx. MK It was in 
pursuance thereof that the workman ioined duty as paid 
apprentice It was thereafter that he was nut on probation 
and finally was confirmed. Keeping in view these facts it 
is difficult to accept that there is any force in the claim of 
the workman The workman could not get the benefit^ of 
his temporary service for the purposes of his regular appoint- 
ment The ex-perte evidence of tfip Management ha^clearN 
established (he contention of the Management. Appointment 
tetter Fx Ml 4 * F abn categorical Similarly his appointment 
letter on probation cony Ex. Ml3 also would co to show 
that the workman now cannot claim the benefit of bis pieviou*, 
service. There is nothing in the De?ai Award or Sastry 


Award or Bipartite Settlement which enables the workman 
the benefit of previous temporary service of 180 days or hi$ 
service as paid appientice for the purposes cf his icgular 
appointment with the bank and in view thereof it would 
follow that the workman is not entitled to anv i die f in this 
refoicucc and the issue is decided accordingly and it is held 
that the action of the Management of Oriental Bank of 
Commerce Ltd., New Delhi in appointing as an apprentice 
w.e.f. 1-5-1971 after 180 days satisfactory temporary service 
in the bank as a clerk is justified and that he is not cnl filed 
to any relief whabso-ever. Parties however would bear theii 
own costs.
