AWARD 

This is a reference under S. 10 of the LD. Act, 1947. The 
Central Government by is notification No, L-12012|79|78-D, II| 
(A/ dated 22nd February, 1979 has referred if. is dispute to 
this Tribunal for adjudication on the following terms ; 

SCHEDULE 

“Wether the action of the management of the State 
Bank of India, Giridih Branch, Dist. Giridih in dis- 
missing Shri M. M. Mondal, Cleark-typist w.e.f. 
16-2-78 is justified 7 If not, to what relief he is en- 
titled T' 

2. Before I proceed with the discussion of this case I would 
like kc briefly place on the record the facts of the case. Shri M. 
M. Mondal the concerned workman happened to be a cleik in 
the Giridih Branch of the State Bank of India and for some 
time he was Assisant Secretary of the Union. On 8-9-76 the 
concerned workman cam© late and h© learnt that the attendance 
register had been sent to the chamber of the Branch Manager. 
He then went to the chamber of the Branch Manager and 
wanted to sign the attendance in the general attendance register. 
The Branch Manager asked hm to sign the attendance in the 
late attendance regiter giving reasons for his late arrival. The 
managements ca^e is that in order to ensure punctuality of 
staff. The system was introduced since June, 1976 by a circu- 
lar and office order under which late arrivals were to sign late 
attendance register and to explain the reasons for coming late. 
Shri Mondal refused to sign the late attendance register and 
insisted that he should be allowed to sign the regular attend- 
ance regiter. The Branch Manager refused to do so where upon 
Shri Mondal began shouting, hurling abuses in filthy language 
and attempted to snatch the attendance register from the hands 
of the manager. In that process the manager was violently 
pushed and he staggered to the wall and got some injury. The 
late attendance register was evenually smirched from the hands 
of the manager and the workman thereafter signed the attend- 
ance register. On 10-9-76 being advised by the head office 
the Branch Manager suspended th© concerned workman and 
he f nriher asked Hm not to come to the office without per- 
mission of the manager, A charge-sheet was issued acainst him 
210 GT/81 — 12 


on 24-9-76 and th© concerned workman submitted his explai 
ation to the charge-sheet on 6-10-76. The concerned workma 
completely denied th© incident and alleged that the manage 
abused him and asked him to get out of his chamber. H 
further said that one of his colleague Shri N. Mukerjee inter 
vened whereupon the manager on his own accord threw th 
attendance register on his table and asked him to sign it 
Shri Mondal then signed the attendance register and went 
This explanation was found to b© unsatisfactory and p domesti 
enquiry was held. The enquiry officer conducted a departmenta 
enquiry and submitted his report to the management o 
21-11-77. The enquiry officer found that the charge o« 
shouting at the manager, abusing the manager in filthy 
language and attempt to snatch the attendance register fron 
the hands of the manager and pushing the manager violently 
against the wall was proved. But in his opinion the charge ol 
assault had not been proved. On his report' the management's 
disciplinaiy authority considered the entire matter and taking 
into consideration the past service record of the concerned 
workman ordered his dismissal by the letter dated 6-2-78* 
After that order the workman filed an appeal before the Ap- 
pellate authority and that appeal was dismissed by order dated 
18-6-78. The workman then raised an industrial dispute under 
S. 2 A of the I. D, Act, and moved the conciliation officer for 
setting aside the order of dismissal and giving him the relief 
of reinstatement On failure of conciliation this reference w«s 
made by the Government of India, Ministry of Labour to fhsl 
Tribunal for adjudication. So far as the domestic enquiry 
is concerned a preliminary question was raised as to whether 
the enquiry was fair and proper. By order dated 11-6-80 Jl 
was found by this Tribunal that the domestic enquiry wtu 
fair and proper. Since then both the parties have been heard 
on merits. We will now discuss as to whether the order of 
dismissal of Shri M. M, Mondal, clerkjtypist with effect from 
16-2-78 is justified. 

3. I would like to reproduce the charge-sheet dated 24th

the 8th October, 1976, 

I have mentioned these two documepts because they arc the 
earliest in connection with this case. It will appear that 
in the evidence there has been an attempt to show that 
Shri Mondal used abusive language, In the charge-sheet 
no mention has been made that Shri Mondal used abusive 
language. But theie is a closer mention that Shri Mondal 
shouted at the Branch Manager. The enquiry officer found 
that there was no satisfactory evidence to prove that Shri 
mondal assaulted the Branch Manager. The charge shows 
thtt Shri Mondal physically pushed the manager towards the 
wail of the chamber. In the evidence the contention is that 
h c received injury due to being pushed against the wall. In 
the charge sheet there is no mention that thp Branch Manager 
received injury. I need not recount the evidence of each and 
every witness of the management. 7 witnesses were examin- 
ed for the management. They are Shri Jasbir Singh, the 


branch manager, Shri D. L. Roy, Sim S. Singhal, Shn 

S. R, Sarkar, Shri S. Banerjec, Shri K. B. Banerjee and Shn 
R. Sehgal. They are consistent in their evidence on the 
point that Shri Mondal shouted at the mmager end in the 
act of snatching the attendance register, pushed the manager 
m a result of which the manager collided with tho wall. 
In the course of argument it was pointed out thai in tho 
evidence of the witnesses of the management there arc certain 
discrepancies with regard to the time of arrival of the wit- 
nesses. Some of them havo been used to signify that a num- 
ber of witnesses were not present at the time of occurence 
in the chamber of the manager. But it has been amply cor- 
roborated by the presence of at least some of the witnesses. 
It is also apparent that a great hue and cry was raised in 
the Chamber of the manager and naturally a good number 
of staff rushed to tho chamber of the manager to see what 
was happening. My nature of the case of both the parties 
It is apparent that some incident took place in the chamber 
of the manager. On his own admission Shri Mondal came a 
little late and hc was required to sign the late attendance 
register. This occurrence took place because Shri Mondal 
wanted to sign the regular register instead of the late atten- 
dance register. It is dear that Shri Mondal did not obey the 
direction of the manager issued to him. Furthermore, Shri 
Mondal shouted at the manager which was not proper on his 
part to do. Probably Shri Mondal thought that he could do 
so as he was the secretly of the union and therefore could 
biow-beat the branch manager. This was further aggravated 
because the manager refused to oblige him by permitting 
him to sign the regular attendance register with die result 
that Shri Mondal deliberately wanted to snatch the atten- 
dance register by force. Shri Ranen Roy, Advocate re- 
presenting the State Bank of India has argued that the 
workman cannot seek shelter on account of the finding ct 
the enquiry officer that the assault story was not proved. 
According to him the very fact that the manager was push- 
ed by Shri Mondal in the act of snatching the register would 
go to constitute assault within the definition provided in the 
Indian Penal Code. I agree that the use of force comes 
within the definition of assault. In the chaige-sbeet the 
manager has clarified as to what he meant by assault which 
h quite consistent with the definition of assault. 

4. It has been pointed out to me that another clerk Shri 
N, Mukhcrjee was similarly charge-sheeted in a separate pro- 
ceeding and was also found guilty. Since Shri Mukherjee 
admitted his guilt and begged forgiveness of the manage- 
ment he wars lightly punished. Shri Mondal had however 
unsatisfactory conduct in the past. There was a proceeding 
against him for charging false T. A- in which he wag punish- 
ed with dismissal, but tho appellate authority reinstated 
him with lighter punishment. This fact was used for the 
purpose of discriminatory attitude on the part of the manage- 
ment. But both the cases have to stand on n senerate toot- 
ing and within the discretion of the management to award 
the quantum of punishment. I therefore Jo not see that this 
is a case which could be struck down on the ground of 
discrimination, 

5. It has further been urged that in the presentation of 
this case and the case against Shri N. Mukherjee there are vital 
di^cripancies. Unfortunately the record of the ease against 
Shri Mukherjee is not before me. Shri Roy has vehemently 
urgued that since Shri Mukherjee is not a party to this 

roceeding, not a word of that proceeding could be used 
y him in this case. I agree that this is so and therefore 
discrepancies pointed out in the course of argument between 
the two proceedings cannot be regarded as relevant for a 
decision in this case, 

6. It is apparent from the above that the chaige against 
Shri Mondal has been amply proved by the evidence a?nd 
circumstances. The management has also considered the pre- 
vious conduct of Shri Mondal in awarding the extreme 
punishment of dismissal. 

7. The main attempt on the part of the concerned work- 
man is to show that the order of dismissal was passed be- 
cause the management victimised him for his trade union 
activities. For this purpose a number of documents wertf 
filed to show that it raised a large number of industrial 
disputes and put forward a number of grievance a of the 
workmen who were members of the union and even made 
complaint against the management in the capacity of being 



[vnr II— Tim 3 (ii)] 




secretary of the recognised union, There it no direct evu 
dence on the point that for these activities the management 
ha\e victimised him. I need not say that the onus to pro\e 
wctimisation lies 011 the workman and the Tnbunal must 
not take the plea of victimisation lightly. Moreover, m 
ca^es where the misconduct has been proved the defence ot 
victimisation cannot be entertained. Since this Is a settled 
law it is not woi th while to quote the number of decisions on 
this point. On behalf of the management it was pleaded 
that the refeicncc was not maintainable. I 11 fact the manage- 
rnent w r anted a decision on this point as a prilimirvaiy issue. 
Blit It was agreed that this point should akso be heard along 
with the merits of the case. 'The State Bank of India, Giridih 
Branch ia an establishment under the Bihar Shops & 
Establishment Act and the workman vShri Mondal was an 
employee as defined under that Act. The workman therefore 
had a right to file the complaint befoie the labour Comt 
under S, 26 of Bihar Shops & Establishment Act foi quash- 
ing the order of dismissal and for getting the oider of re- 
instatement, It has been conceded on behalf of the work- 
men that under S. 26 of Bihar Shops & Establishment Act 
the concerned workman could seek the above relief in the 
Labour Court. In fact such a complaint was raised theie 
and had been withdiawn, whereafter an* industrial dispute 
was raised by the concerned workman himself and the re- 
ference was made under S.2A of I be LD. Act. Sri Ranen 
Roy has argued that since the Bihar Shops & Establishment 
Act got the assent of the President of Tndin the provisions 
of the Bihar Shop and Establishment Act would be applicable 
in Bihar In supersession of the I. D. Act. The Full Bench 
of Andhra Predcsh High Court has dealt with this question 
S. 41 of the Madras Shops & Establishment Act is identical 
to the provisions of S. 26 of Bihar Shops & Establishment 
Act, It was held in the Andhra Pradesh ca^e that S, 41 
of the Madras Act gives complete relief to an Individual 
dismissed employee in the matter of reinstatement. S. 10 
and S. 15 read with S. 2A of the 1. D. Act also gives the 
same relief. Therefore so far as individual workman Is con- 
cerned in the case of dismissal, he can seek relief only under 
S. 41 of the Madras Act and cannot raise an Industrial dis- 
pute under S. 2 A of the T. D. Act. The reference made 
under S. 2 A by the Government of India was held in that 
decision to be without jurisdiction and was quashed. This 
decision is reported in 1977, Vol. II TJJ-332, Tt was followed 
by a decision reported in 1978 Vol. II LLJ-2K7. It was held 
that while an individual workman could not seek relief under 
S, 2 A of the I. D, Act, the same bar could not apply to a 
union or a body of workmen who have no locus-stand i under 
the Shops Act. In he instant case the reference is under 
S. 2 A and therefore both these decisions were placed before 
me to show that the reference made by the Government of 
[ndia in the instant case is without jurisdiction. For the 
purpose of jurisdiction Shri T. P. Ohoudhury, Advocate re- 
presenting the workman has placed reliance on a decision 
of the Patna High Comt as reported in 1972 Vol, II LLJ 
395, Tt waa a Full Bench decision. The question involved 
was as to whether S. 26 of the Bihar Shops & Establish- 
ment Act and the 1. D. Act are repugnant to each ofher and 
therefore S. 26 of the Shops Act becomes void, The Full 
Bench held that they w^ere not repugnant as a while because 
the scope and mandet of the 1. D. Act ^nd Bihar Shops & 
Establishment Act are different. The discussion of the Full 
Bench judgement will show that such matters as w-e have 
before us were also considered and the Court was of the 
view that the jurisdiction was co-cxtensive. Since we have to 
he guided by the Patna decision in preference to Andhia 
Pradesh decision, T have to hold that Ibis reference is main- 
tainable and not without jurisdiction, 

8. In view of my findings above, T have no ample reason 
to interfere with the order of dismissal passed by the manage- 
ment of State Bank of Tndia, Giridih. Accordingly, T hold 
that the action of the management of the Slate Bank of 
India, Giridih Branch Dist. Giridih in dumisdn-z Shri 
M. M. Mondal, Clerktypist with effect from 16-2-78 is 
iustfied. Consequently, Shri M. M, Mondal is not entitled 
to any relief. 

This is my award. 

J. P. SINGH, Piesiding Officer,

STATE : West Bengal INDUSTRY : Banking 

AWARD 

This is a reference under Section 10 of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, 1947 sent to this Tribunal by the Government of India, by 
virtue of its Order No. F. No. L-12012/S0/77-D. 1T.A dated 
5th April, 1978 for adjudication of an industrial dispute bet- 
ween the employee in relation to the management of the Union 
Bank of Tndia, Calcutta, hereinafter referred to as the “Bank” 
and their workman represented by the General Secretary, 
Bengal Bank Workeis Organisation, fl-Kiron Sankar Roy Road, 
Calcutta, hereinafter referred to as the “Union' 1 . The dispute 
has been formulated in the Schedule to Ihe Reference in the 
following words : 

“Whether the action of the Union Bank of Tndia, 15-India 
Exchange Place^ Calcutta in withdrawing their re- 
presentative Shri B. K. Nag from the Clearing House 
at Calcuia and depriving him of the Special Assistants 
allowance is legal and justified ? If not to what relief 
is the woikman entitled ?" 

2. Both the parlies appeared in this case and filed their 
respective written statements and there is also a rejoinder on 
the side of the Union. 

3, To be brief, the case of ihe Union is that Ihe concerned 
woikman B. K. Nag was in the clerical cadre. He was allotted 
the duties of Clearing House representative of the Bank at 
Reserve Bank of India and the said duties of Clearing House 
representative attracted the special allowance of Ra. 91/- per 
month in accordance with the provisions of paragraph 5.2. 
and 5.6 of the Bipartite Settlement dated 19lb October, 1966. 
At first the concerned workman was not paid any srecial 
allowance but aftei the decision of the Supreme Court he vas 
given special allowance for his woiking as representative at 
the Hearing Hcmse Therenflc? Ihe Bank took B. K Nag to 
the Branch office after taking him away from the assignment 
as Hearing House lrnrcscntalive and thus the woikman was 
dimmed of the benefit of special allowance According to the 
Union this tiansfei from the Clearing House w^as mnlafide. 
The v\o kman was entitled to be designated as Special A^sis- 
Unt-cum-Clerk bv the management and he should have been 

lowed to wnik as renre^en Native of the Bank Ac he worked 
foi’ a period of moi> than thiee years as the Bank's represen- 
tative Ov Hearing House he should not have been de- 
nrivrd of thr special allowance. The prayer of the Union is 
ifrt the withdrawal of the concerned workman from the 
Clearing House and drpimnp him of the special allowance 


210 GT/8I — 13 



1844 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JUNE 6, 1981/JYAISTHA 16, 1903 


[Fart II— Sec. 3(il)] 


lied, for a direction upon the Bank to allot duties of special 
assistant to the concerned workman and for a direction upon 
the Bank to reimburse the loss sustained by B. K. Nag due to 
malafide transfer. 

4. According to the Bank's case the reference is not main- 
tainable as the Union does net represent the workmen of the 
Bank and that the Union has no authority to aspouse the cause 
of the concerned workman. It is also alleged that according to 
the provisions of the Bipartite Settlement dated 19th October, 
1966 the concerned workman is entitled to get special allow- 
ance so Jong as he discharges the duties of the special assistant. 
The workman was in clerical cadre and he could be. withdrawn 
from the Clearing House of the Reserve Bank and when he 
ceased to do any additional work or duty of any special assis- 
tant as representative of the Clearing House besides his own 
duties ns clerk, he could not claim special allowance. The Bank 
is at liberty to transfer the workman to different assignments as 
clerk and he cannot as a matter of right claim to be posted as 
representative of the Clearing House and to get special allow- 
ance. The workman was not a special assistant and the Bank 
acted legally when he was taken away from his assignment as 
representttfive of the Bank at the Clearing House. There was no 
malice or malafidcs in the conduct of the Bank. According to 
the Bank the workman concerned can get np relief, 

5. To-dflv was fixed for hearing of the case. On the last 
date the Union did not appear and no step was taken on the 
side of the Union. To-day also the Union has taken no step for 
its absence. The Bank is present with evidence. The Tribunal 
waited for the Union up to 12 Noon. Then the case was taken 
up. The Bank examined one witness J.P. Narayan. He is at pre- 
Kent Pc: sonnel Officer in the Zonal office of the Bank at 
Madras. At the relevant time he was the Personnel Officer in 
Calcutta Zonal office. From him we get that B.K. Nag the 
concerned workman was a clerk. That was his substantive 
post. For a period the concerned workman acted as representa- 
tive of the Bank at the Clearing House and at that time beside* 
his own ordinary duties as clerk he performed some additional 
duties for which he was given special allowance according to 
the Bipartite Settlement in view of the Supreme Court deci- 
sion. We also get that the concerned workman was never 
appointed as Special Assistant. He was wihdrawn from, the 
assignment of the representative at the Clearing House and 
was taken back to his own duties as clerk at the branch of the 
Bank and thereafter he ceased to do any duty of the Special 
Assistant. He had no responsibility of the Special Assistant and 
sis such the Bank did hot allow the concerned workman spe- 
cial allowance. From tho witness we also get that the Bank 
has its own union which is known as Union 
Bank of Tndia Employees Association, West Bengal. 
Almost all the employees of the Bank in West Bengal are 
members of the said Union. The witness has further stated 
that the Bengal Bank Workers Organisation is not a union of 
the Bank and the said union has no right to represent tho 
workmen of the Bank. 

6. The first question that arises as preliminary point 
regarding the maintainability of tho reference. We get that the 
Bank has its own union and that almost all the employees of 
the Bank in West Bengal are members of the said union. We 
also get that the Bengal Bank Workers Organisation is not a 
union of the Bank and that it has no right to represent the 
workmen of the Bank. The union does not come to prove that 
it is the representative body and that it has got right to represent 
the workmen of the Bank and to espouse or take up the cas® 
of tho workman. The evidence is one sided and in favour of 
the Bank. It is therefore, clear that the Union at whose in- 
stance the reference has been made and which wants to re- 
present the workmen of the Bank has no locus standi or right 
to do the same, According to the Industrial Disputes Act, the 
present dispute is not an industrial, dispute. The preliminary 
point, therefore, goes in favour of the Bank and in that view of 
the matter tho reference is incompetent. 

7. fn order to make the award complete, T may also deal 
with the merits of the clajm mode by the Union. The Union 
relies unon paragraphs 5.2 and 5,6 of the Bipartite Settlement 
dated 19-10-1966, When the two paragraphs are read together 
It is quit© clear that an employee in the clerical cadre as In the 
present case is entitled to get special allowance for the perfor- 
mance or discharge of certain additional duties and functions 
over and above the routine duties and functions of the said 
workman. For getting that allowance the workman should con- 
tinue to perform the duties whole time and not casnallv or 
occasionally. Paragraph 5.9 savs that the workman would be 
entitled to special allowance only so lone as he is in charge of 
such work or performance of such duties attracting special 


allowance. Now, the duties to be performed for getting special 
allowance have been mentioned in Appendix B’ to the Bipartite 
Settlement, For a clerk to get special assistant’s special allow- 
ance he has to do some duties or any of them as mentioned in 
item, (xix) of Part I in Appendix B. In the present case B, K, 
Nag the concerned workman was in the clerical cadre and as 
clerk when he was assigned to work as representative of the Bank 
at the Clearing House he was to do certain duties as mentioned 
in item No. (xix) in Part I of Appendix B over and above hi* 
routine and ordinary duties os clerk. This assignment as re- 
presentative of the Bank was given to him and ho performed 
the duties as clerk doing some additional duties as mentioned 
in item No, (xix). He was not promoted as Special Assistant. 
Clearly according to the terms of service a cloarh can be trart- 
ferred from one office to another and from one assignment *o 
another, of course as a clerk. The clerk can not, as a matter 
of right, claim that he should have been allowed to tontine© 
in the post where special allowance was given. In the present 
case B. K. Nag was taken back to the branch of the Bank a' 
clerk and he ceased to be the represent'd of 'he Bank at Ihr* 
Clearing House and he did not do any duty as mentioned in 
ilrm (xix) of Part T of Appendix B of the Bipartite Settlement. 
Therefore, the claim of the Unim workm in shea id bt 

reallotted duties as representative of the Bank or that he should 
be designated ns Special Assistant-cum-cleark, which is the crea- 
tion of the Union itself because there is no such office in the 
Bank, is unacceptable. There is no evidence of malafidcs on the 
part of the Bank when the workman was withdrawn from the 
Clearing House. The action of the Bank in withdrawing B. K. 
Nng from the Clearing House is quite legal and justified. 
There is no question of depriving him of the special as$lrtant> 
allowance. As. B K. Nag was not required to do any additional 
duties besides his own, he f* not entitled to get any special 
allowance. 

8. I, therefore, find in view of my discussions above that tho 
workman concerned is not entitled to get any relief either on 
preliminary point or on merit. 

This is my award. 

Calcutta, 

Dated, The 11th May. 1981. 

R. BHATTACHARYA, Presiding Officer

entitled 7 

2. On receipt of the reference it was ordered to be regis- 

tered but before any proceedings could take place this 
reference was transferred to Labour Court (I), Kanpur 
where it was registered afresh and a statement cf claim 
was Hied by the workman, Before an v further proceedings 
could take place the case was transferred to Industrial 
Tribunal, Delhi and finally the case was transferred to this

Govt, in 1979. Even though a written statement had 

already been llled by the Management and a replication 
was also hied by the workman but in view of fresh or*Jer

1979: 

ISSUES: 

1. Whether the Inquiry is void for the grounds alleged? 

2. What is the effect of non exhaustion of Departmen- 

tal remedies on this reference. 

3. As In order of reference. 

3, Thereafter evidence of the parties was recorded. Evi- 
dence of the workman consists of his statement on affidavit 
as W.W 1. The said affidavit is Ex. A|l. While the evi- 
dence the Management consists of the statement on

1|7 He has also tendered Ex. M.W.l |8 which is the order

cross examinations of either W.W.l or M.W.l. However 
reference would be made thereto as and when necessary. 

4. It is in the light of this evidence and the pleadings that 

this case has to be considered I have gone through the 

evidence produced by the parties and have heard their 

representatives at length and after giving my considered 
thought to the mutter before me 1 have come to the follow- 
ing findings upon these issues: 

ISSUE NO. 1. 

5. The contention of the workman is that he had joined 

the services of the Union Bank of India at Jabalpur Branch 
as a cashier on 3-5-66 and he was promoted as Head 
Cashier on 13-6 70 and was posted at Aligarh Branch; f h.it 
at Aligarh Branch a chaigc sheet dated 10-7-72 was served 
upon him in pursuance whereof he w r as also suspended and 
an enquiry was held against him. After the enquiry his 

services were terminated; that the enquiry was vitiated for 

the. reasons alleged in the statement of claim which I will 
presently examine 

6. The Management has in its written statement admitted 
his employment and his promotion and service of a charge 
sheet and consequent suspension of this workman, holding 
of an enquiry and ultilate dismissal of this workman but 
it has been alleged by the management that none of the 
grounds challenging the^ validity of the enquiry holds any 
ground and as such it is urged that the enquiry was pro- 
perly held in accordance with the principle of natural 
justice and was not void. 

7. It has been urged on behalf of the workman that the 
order of his suspension was invalid However from the 
perusal of order of suspension which Ex. W|53 I find thU 
there is no weight in this contention of the workman The 
representative of the work' an has referred to rule 19.12.(6). 
From the perusal thereof I find that there is nothing . m 
para 19. 12(b) which invalidates the order of suipendon 


Ex. W|53. Last para of Ex. W]53 reads that ‘since the 
Charges levelled againd Mr. drived! aie of serious nature 
pending tuuher enquiry* Mr. luvedi is suspended fiom ibe 
Bank’s service with immediate effect.’ Dui mg the period of 
his suspension Mr. Irivedi w T ill be entiLlod to subsistancc 
allowance as per the rules,’ 

8, Para 19.12(b) provides that‘ pending such enquiry he 

may be suspended ' There i* absolutely no conflict 

in the p.ovisions of para 19.12(b) and last para ol Lire 
order of suspension h\. W|53 and in view thereof I hold 
that thcic is no weight in this contention of the workman. 
U js further uiged that a freffi charge sheet Ex, W|49 wn 3 
served upon the workman on 28-12-72 but mere fact that 
a fresh charge sheet Ex. W|49 was served later on would 
not ctlect the suspension order. In fact Ex. W|53 is in the 
form of a memorandum In the form of a show came call- 
ing out circumstances which have necessitated a disciplinary 
action and an enquiry against this workman while Ex. 
W]49 is the formal charge sheet. There is no conflict bet- 
ween Ex. W[49 and Ex W[53 and therefore it cannot bo 
said that the order of suspension was invalid, Even other- 
wise mere fact that the order of suspension was invalid would 
not in any manner effect the enquiry proceedings. The en- 
quiry proceedings have to be seen independently of order 
of suspension. The enquiry can be vitiated only if it is 
shown that principles of natural justice were not complied 
with or it would be vitiated because of mis-carriage of jus- 
tice and not be cause there was some defect in the order 
of suspension. In view thereof T hold that the order of 
suspension was not invalid and does not suffer from any 
lacunae and aho does not adverslv effect the enquiry pro- 
ceedings or the enquiry or the findings. 

9> The next^ objection taken before me against the en- 
quiry is that it was a fore© in as much as Enquiry Officer 
and the punishing authority were the same. But the repie- 
sentntivc of the workman ha? not been able to show 
anything which goes to show that there is any bar that 
the Enquiiy Officer and the Punishing officer cannot be the 
same poison. Rather my attention on behalf of the Bank 
has been drawn to para 19.14 which goes to show that 
the Enquiry Officer and the punishing authority can be the 
same person as w r ell. Even otherwise from the perusal of 
the enquiiy proceedings and the orders passed ultimately 
bv the Fnquiry Officer I do not find that any prejudice has 
been caused to the workman by the fact that the Enquiiy 
Officer and the punishing authority happen to be the same 
person and in the absence of any prejudice it also cannot 
be said that it has resulted in any injustice to the work- 
man, In view thereof I hold that this objection does not 
hold anv water and docs not vitiate the enquiiy in any man- 
ner what-so-ever, 

10. It has then been urged by the representative of the 
workman that the charge sheeting authority and the authority 
which has confirmed the punishment happen to be the same. 
From the perusal of Ex. W|53 and Ex, W|49 I find that the 
charge sheet has been issued by the Deputy General Manager 
and admittedly it is the Deputy General Manager who bad 
confirmed the order of termination of services of this work- 
man but Id. representative of the workman has not been 
able to draw my attention to any para in either the Desal 
Award or the Bipartite Settlement which lays clown that the 
charge sheeting authority and the authority confirming the 
punishment cannot be the same. It is not that the punishing 
authority and the confirming authority are the same in the 
instant case. Punishing authority is altogether different from 
the authority which has confirmed the punishment. In view 
thereof it cannot be said that there is any force in thig con- 
tention of the workman and as such it cannot be said that 
this fact alone has the effect of invalidity the enquiry. 

11, The next objection of the workman side that the En- 
quiry Officer was a Personnel Officer and was subordinate to 
the charge sheeting authority and such the enquiry is viti- 
ated, There is no force in this contention. There is nothing 
in the Bank award or the Bipaitite settlement which required 
that the Fnquiry Officer shall not be a person subordinate to 
the charge sheeting authority, It also cannot be said that this 
in Itself was against the principles of natural^ justice. Keeping 
in view the scope of the domestic enquiry it is inharent there- 
in that the Enquiry Officer would invariably be a person 
subordinate to the disciplinary authority. A perusal of para 
19,14 would show that the powers have been conferred upon 
the Chief Executive Officer or the Principal Officer or an 



1846 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA ; JUNE 6, 1981/JYAISTHA 16, 1903 LEvar 11— Sec. 3(ii)J 


alternative officer at the Head office of Principal officer appoint- 
ed by him to appoint an Enquiry Officer. Obviously ho can 
bo competent to deputing only his subordinate and not 
person not amenable to his administrative powers to hold the 
enquiiy. In these circumstances I do not find any weight m 
this conteniion either and hold that this also does not vitiate 
the enquiry. 

12. It has next been urged that the workman was not afford- 
ed full oppoitumLy ior representing himself by the Enquiry 
Officer. However n is admitted by the workman during his 
cross-examination as W.W.l that it was coirect that he was 
repiesented by Air. Aljshia and Jam his nominee in this be- 
half before the Enquiry Officer, This shows that the workman 
had been afforded full opportunity to represent or to be le- 
presented by his representative before the Enquiry Officer. 
Even otherwise from the perusal of enquiry piocecdings 
which are Ex. M.W.1|2 1 iind that invariably the workman 
was represented by his representative at all hearings. He was 
always present as well. Almost each page of the enquiry 
piocecdings is signed by the workman or his leprescntatfve. 
That being the position it would be difficult to accept that 
the workman had not been afforded full opportunity for 
representing his case before the Enqunv Officer. 

13. It is then urged that Lhc workman has not been afforded 
full oppoitunity to cross examine the witnesses. However a 
rcfcicnce to die statement ol M.W.l, Shri S, k, Ajsgarwal 
would show that it is categorically stated by him during cross 
examination that 1 it may be that certain it relevant question 
were disallowed but otherwise full oportunity was given to 
the workman and his representatives to cross examine the 
witnesses of the Management.' 1 have perused the entiie 
enquiry proceedings Ex. M.W.l [2 and from the perusal thereof 
1 find that all the witnesses of the respondent weie cross 
examined on behalf of the workman and wherever the En- 
quiiy Officer did not find proper to allow question in the cross 
examine, a formal order was passed by him. From the peru- 
sal of the enquiry proceedings it cannot be said that while 
dishallowing cross examination occasionally the enquiry 
officer has mis-dirccted himself or acted malafidcly and in view 
thereof 1 hold that there is no weight in this contention of 
the workman. In this context my special attention was drawn 
hi pages 33, 34 and of the enquiiy proceedings but a perusal 
of these pages even does not go to establish that the action 
of the Enquiry Officer In dishallowing those Questions was 
not justified in as much as these questions were not strictly 
relevant for the purposes of enquiry* rather these questions 
had a tendency to scandalise and as such the Enquiry Offi- 
cer was justified in dis-al lowing these questions.^ The net 
result is that the enquiry cannot be held to be vitiated on 
this ground either. 

14. It has then been urged that newspaper cutting Ex. 22 
and Ex. 23 were not allowed to be put to the witness. How- 
ever these documents cannot be connected with the case of 
the workman. They are not directly relevant to the enquiry 
and in the absence thereof they cannot be permitted to be 
placed on record and exibited and J do not see anything 
wrong in the action of the Enquiry Officer in this behalf and 
hold that the enquiry proceedings are not vitiated for this 
ground either. 

15. It has then been urged upon charge no, 3 that relevant 
letter and couplet were not placed before the Enquiry Officer 
but this is not born out from the record. The cnplet is essen- 
tially an annexure of letter Ex. 15. The workman has ad- 
mitted the enclosure to Ex. 15 in his hand writing and under 
his signatures, as a reference to page 85 of enquiry proceed- 
ings would show The Enquiry Officer has categorically stated 
in his affidavit Ex M.W.1|1 in para 7 that the documents hs 
contained in the enquiry file were actually produced before 
him. In the face of all these facts it cannot be said that the 
relevant letter or couplet was not produced before (he Enquiry 
Officer and therefore it follows that the enquiry is not vitiated 
on this ground, In this context it has also been urged that the 
Enquiry Officer at page 6 of Ex. M.W.l |3 has given a finding 
which is not born out from the evidence on record but T 
have already held above that the couplet was an enclosure 
to letter Ex 15 and the Enquiry Officer was justified to relv 
upon that in arriving at his conclusions and findings ami 
therefore it cannot be said that finding at page 6 of Fx. 
M W ll3 me not based on evidence and in view thereof this 
objection is held to have no force and cannot vitiate the 
enquiry. 


16. It has also been urged before me ou behalf of the 
Workman charge no. 5 in Ex. 5 regarding the mis-behaviour 
with the Accountant is not established in view of the diffe- 
rence in the dates of the incident. Horn the perusal of the 
evidence befoie the Enquiry Officer, it would be found that 
the incident he has admitted that on 23rd June, he was hit by 
an iron gate and had lequested Mr, Mishra, the accountant 
to get the iron gate tied with a rope, That being the position 
it cannot be said that the finding upon charge no. 5 are not 
warranted merely becuusc there is some difference as to the 
dates. 

17. It has been urged that the Enqiiy Officer had mis- 
dn toted hjmselt by acting piejudicially and in biased manner 
bin the leprescntauve of the workman has not been able to 
bear this tact out ol the enqimy iccoid, We have to go by 
the enquiry recoid. Each page of the enquiry proceedings 
bears the signatures of the workman, I hat being the position 
noihmg which does not find place on the enquiry record can 
be accepted at this late stage an^i it cannot therefore be said 
that the Enquiry Officer was biased against the workman or 
hud mis-Juccied himself. From the pciusal of entire enquiry 
lecord it cannot be gathered that the enquiry officer had 
acted in a biased manner or had mis-directed himseif or had 
remeted in mis-carriage of the justice. Meie fact that now 
and then questions were put by Enquiry officer to elicit ans- 
wcis would not itself invalidate the enquiry. Merc fact that the 
Enquiry Officer disallowed certain irrelevant questions dur- 
ing cross examination would also not go to show that Em 
quay Officer had acted in a biased manner or had mis-directed 
himseli or the enquiry. It also cannot be said that Enquiry 
Officer had in any manner acted as a piosccutor, The bank 
was duly represented by its representative all throughout 
before the Enquiry Officer and therefore it cannot be said 
that the Fnquiiy Officer had mis-directcd himself or the en- 
quiry or had acted in a biased or prejudicial manner oi that 
in any manner what-so-ever the conduct of the enquiry offi- 
cer had prejudicially effected the workman. I have gone 
through the relevant documents as also the statements of va- 
rious. witnesses before the Enquiry Officer and after giving my 
considered thought, thereto I am of the opinion that it cannot 
be said that the findings and conclusions of the Enquiry Offi- 
cer were pei verse or were such as no reasonable man would 
arrive at and (n the face thereof it cannot be said that the 
findings of the Enquiry Officer are vitiated, It also cannot be 
said that the findings are not borne out from the evidence 
on record. It also cannot be said that the Enquiry Officer 
has ignored any ev Wiener produced before me. The accumul- 
ative effect of my discussions above is that there is nothing 
which has been established to exist which vitiates the enquiry 
proceedings in the instant case. The enquiry has been 
held in due observance of principles of natural justice. Every 
opportunity was afforded to the workman to present his 
case. He had been afforded first an opportunity to explain in 
pursuance of Ex, Ml 53 and thereafter formal charge sheet 
Ex, M|49 was served upon the workman and thereafter en- 
quiry was held by M.W.l Ex. R|1 is the order appointing 
Shri S. K, Aggarwal as the Enquiry Officer. The order is cate- 
gorical and is dated 12-1-1972. It also cannot be said that 
any new charges have been added in Ex, Ml 49 after the 
service of Ex. M|53, The workman was afforded an opportu- 
nity after the Enquiry Officer had arrived at his findings, 
opportunity to show cause against the proposed punishment 
after the Enquiry Officer had arrived at his findings. It was 
only after he had been heard that the order of punishment 
was passed. The appeal of the workman addressed to the 
Dy, Generali Manager was also considered and disposed of, 
From whichever angle the matter may be considered T do not 
find anything to even remotely suggest that the enquiry in 
the instant case was vitiated or that it was liable to be set 
aside. It may be mentioned here that unless the findings of 
the Enqulrv Officer are held to be perverse and not born out 
of ihe evidence befoie me or based upon conjectures this 
court would not super imnose its own findings upon the 
findings of the Enmifrv Officer, From the perusal of various 
documents oin record it would be found that the language 
used bv the workman was intempeiant and did not become of 
a gentleman narticularly in the establishment where theie 
were women employees as well. In view thereof T hold that 
the enouirv is not vitiated on any of tbe grounds alleged 
against it and accordingly issue no, 1 is decided against the 
workman and in favour of the Management. 

Is9r° No. 2 • 

17. In view of mv findings upon Issue no 1, this issue be- 
come redundant. Tn any case it is admitted that an appeal had

been preferred by the workman which was rejected and for 
that reason X hold that this reference is not vitiated by the 
alleged nomexoriion of departmental remedies by the 
workman. 


The Chief Engineer, 

B.S.L. Project, Sundemagar Township Respondent. 

AWARD 


Isssue No. 3 : 

18. In view of my findings upon issue no, 1 I hold that the 
Management of Union Bank of India is justified in dismis- 
sing from service w.e.f, 18th June, 1974 Shri B. R, Trivedi, 
Head Cashier, Aligarh Branch and that ihc workman was 
not entitled to any relief whatsoever. In this context I 
would like to icfcr to the charges levelled against the woik- 
man. Charge no. 1 was that on 16Lh June, 1972 at about 
11.00 AM “Mr. Tiivedi (workman) entered the cabin of Mr. 
N, L, Sharma, Bianch Manager and behaved with him in a 
rude and arrogant manner. In charge no. 2 workman was 
charged with having abused and threatened the Branch 
Manager and attempted to assault the Branch Manager with 
a wooden tray. Under charge no. 3 Mr, Trivedi had been 
charged with asking on 12-6-72 Mr. P. K. Sharma, another 
employee In the bank, to handover a letter to Miss Pushpa 
Kaul in which paper contained indiscieet writing addressed to 
the lady staff member. Charge no. 4 and 6 were dropped. Jn 
charge no. 5 Mr. Trivedi has been charged with approaching 
on 23-6-72 at 3.30 PM the accountant with a complaint re- 
garding the iron gate and behaved in an indecent and im- 
moral manner in the presence of other staff member and on 
being advised by the accountant to behave in a decent and 
proper manner, Mr. Trivedi started abusing the accountant. 
Keeping in view the nature of the charges it cannot be said 
that the punishment of dismissal from service was unwarrant- 
ed and is not justified. Even a reference to Ex. W|53 which 
reproduced certain language used by the workman also do 
not go to show that the punishment of dismissal is not war- 
ranted in the instant case. Even a perusal of Ex. M/1 the 
enclosure of Ex. 15 -would show that the workman was haidly 
a person deserving a retention in the service in an establish- 
ment where women employees were working. The couplet 
written on Ex. M|1 tends to violate the modesty of any wo- 
man more so of a respectable employee in a banking estab- 
lishment and keeping in view all these facts I do not think 
that the order of termination of services of the workman is 
unjustified and in view of my discussions and findings above, 
it is awarded that the Management of Union Bank of India 
in terminating the services of Shri B, R. Trivedi was justified 
w,ei t 18-6-74 from the post of Head Cashier, Aligarh Branch 
and the workman is not entitled to any relief. However, in 
the circumstances of the case X leave the parties to bear their 
own costs. 

Further ordered : 

That requisite number of copies of this award may be 
sent to the appropriate Govt, for necessary action at their 
end. 

Dated : the 13th April, 1981.

S.O. 1692, — In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act ,1947 (14 of 1947), tbe Central Government 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govern- 
ment Industrial Tribunal, New Delhi, in the industrial dispute 
between the employers in relation to the management .of 
B S L. Project and their workman Shri Raj Paul, which was 
received by he Central Government on the 16th May, 1981. 

BEFORE SHRI MAHESH CHANDRA. PRESIDING OFFI- 
CER, CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBU- 
NA L-CUM-L A B OUR COURT, NEW DELHI 

nx No, 16 of 1980 


In re : 

Shri Rai Paul, Ex, Time Keeper, 
Kothi No. 3302, Sector 35-D, 
Chandigarh. 


STATE: Punjab. 


. . , .Petitioner. 


The Central Government as appropriate Government vide 
its order No, L-42012(33)/79-D.lI(B) dated the 26th March, 
1980 referred an Indusrtial Dispute u fa 10 of the l.P, Act, 
1947 to this Tribunal in the following terms ; 

‘Whether the action of the management of B.S.L. Project 
P.O, Sundemagar Township (HP) in terminating the 
services of Shri Raj Paul under their letter No. 
53009/20 Emergency dated 30-11-76 is justified? 
Jf not, to what relief the workman is entitled.' 

2. On receipt of the reference usual notices were sent to 
the parties but none has appeared for the workman side and 
as such cx-purte proceedings were ordered against the work- 
man, The BSL has put in appearance and filed a statement 
of claim. In view of the statement of claim filed by the BSL 
following issues were framed vide my order dated 5-12-80 
and case was adjourned for ex-parle evidence of the BSL. 
Ex-Parte statement of BSL consists of Shri O. P, Yamdagni in 
tbe manner of an affidavit which is Ex. M/1 and documents 
Ex. M/2 to Ex. M/7. 1 have gone through the documents 
produced by the Management and also the evidence and 
after giving my considered thought to the mutter before me 
I have come to the following findings ; 

Issues : 

L As in the order of reference? 

2. Whether the Indusrtial Dispute is not tenable 7 

Issue No. I : 

According to the statement of claim filed by the Manage- 
ment Raj Paul was employed as a Time Keeper with the res- 
pondent and his services were terminated in as much as he 
was irregular in attendance and his CRs also did not warrant his 
continuance in the service. It is further stated in the state- 
ment of claim by the BSL that Raj Paul was a Time Keeper 
and was not a workman in as much as he was governed by 
Civil service rules and as such this reference was bad. 

3, In order to support its contention the Management has 
filed an affidavit of Superintending Engineer which Is Ex. 
M/1 and from the perusal thereof I find that by and large 
the contention of the Management raised in the statement of 
claim Fas been repealed In the said affidavit Ex. M/1. Ex. 
M/2 is the notice served upon Shri Raj Paul by the Executive 
Engineer on 21-5-70 warning him to be careful in future, Ex. 
M/if is the copy of report for 1975-76 of this workman. E_x, 
M/4 is the notice on the basis of the report Ex. M/3 issued to 
the workman on 4-11-76. Ex. M/5 is the receipt thereof. Ex. 
M/6 is another report for the period June, 1976 to December, 
1976 immediately before his services were terminat- 
ed and Ex. M/7 is copy of d.o. letter dated 25-8-75. 
From the perusal of the affidavit of BSL it cannot be said 
that Shri Raj Paul was not a workman as alleged by BSL. 
Admittedly Shri Raj Paul was working as a Time Keeper. It 
is also admitted that the respondent is an Industry. That being 
the position mere fact that Raj Paul was governed by Civil 
Service Rules would not exclude him from the category of a 
workman. Keeping in view the designation of Rai Paul who 
was a Time Keeper it would essentially follow that he was 
concerned with the charge staff and was part of work charge 
staff. As long as he was a part of work charge staff he would 
be a workman. There is nothing to suggest that he was 
working in a supervisory capacity or that his wages were more 
than Rs. 500 p.M, in that supervisory capacity. Keeping 
in view all these facts it would follow that the conclusion 
would be irresistable that Rai Paul was a workman. A cyclo- 
styled Judgment of Hon’ble Supreme Court of India in Writ 
Petition No. 3598, 43f>0. 4429, 4536, 4391. 4505, 4376, 
4658 of 1978 and 565 of 1979 passed on 29-8-79 entitled as 
Taswant Singh and others Vs. Union of India and others has 
been filed by the Management, From the perusal of this copy 
itself it would be clear that the entire strength of labour emp- 
loyed for the Project was work charge and as such they were 
governed by Ihe movisions of Industrial Dispute Act, 1947. 
Once it la held that the workman was governed bv the ptovF 
sions of Industrial Dispute Act it would follow further that the 
provisions of Section 25-F would be equally applicable to the 
sions of Industrial Dispute Act it would follow further that the 
in sei vice of tbe respondent — BST for a period of more than 


Versus 



1848 


r P art II—SlC. 3 (if.] 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JUNE 6, 1981/JYAISTHA 16, 1903 


one year and there is nothing to suggest that this workman was 
paid any retrenchment compensation within the meaning of 
Section 25-F of I.D. Act, This man was in service with the 
respondent w.e.f. 2-11-67 and remained in service till 1976 
and his services were terminated w.e.f. 30-11-76. He was not 
paid any retrenchment compensation and hence in view of the 
principles of law laid down in N. Sundermany '3 case by the 
Hon’ble Supreme Court of India termination of his services 
would be illegal. Mere fact that the service rules applicable 
to this workman provided that his services could be terminated 
without notice at any time does not in any manner do away 
with the requirement of payment of lelrenchmcnt compensa- 
tion and hence termination was in violation of law and there- 
fore illegal and this issue is decided accoidingly.

Issue No. 2.1 

In view of findings upon issue No. 1 that Shri Rai Paul 
was a workman and provisions of I.D. Act were applicable to 
him it cannot be said that the reference is not maintainable 
and accordingly this issue is also decided against the Manage- 
ment. 

4. For my discussions and findings above, it is awaided 
that the termination of services of this workman Shri Rai Paul 
was not justified and accordingly the workman is entitled to be 
reinstated with full back wages and it is accordingly awarded, 
However parties would bear their own costs.

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 28th May, 1981 

S.O 1693 — Whereas an industrial dispute exists between 
the employers in relation to the management of Bombay Port 
Trust, Bombay and their workmen represented by BPT General 
Workers’ Union, 

And whereas the said employers and their workmen have 
by a written agreement under sub section ( 1 ) cf section 10A of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), agreed to refer 
the said dispute to arbitration and have forwarded to the Cen- 
tral Government under sub-section (3) of section 10 A of the 
said Act, a copy of the said arbitration agreement. 

Now, therefore, in pursuance of sub-scction (1) of section 
10A of the said Act, the Central Government hereby publishes 
the said agreement which was received by it on the 1 2th May 
1981
